> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Differential reasoning, discriminators and investigation logic are reliable at intern level. **Every dose, threshold, size cut-off, duration definition and reference range carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** The Fleischner Society nodule follow-up intervals in 0.7 in particular must be read from the source — they are size-, risk- and morphology-stratified and are not reconstructable from memory.
>
> Management of the specific conditions named here sits in [[F0.5]] (asthma, COPD, pneumonia, pulmonary oedema) and [[A3]] (respiratory failure). This file is about the undifferentiated presentation.

---

## 0.1 Acute Dyspnoea

**D:** Subjective awareness of uncomfortable breathing developing over minutes to days.

**A/P:** Dyspnoea arises from a mismatch between the respiratory drive generated centrally and the ventilation actually achieved — sensed through chemoreceptors, pulmonary stretch and irritant receptors, and chest wall mechanoreceptors. **This is why it is not a purely respiratory symptom**: metabolic acidosis, anaemia and anxiety all generate it through the same final pathway.

> [!tip] Time course narrows the differential faster than anything else
> **Seconds:** pneumothorax, pulmonary embolism, inhaled foreign body, arrhythmia.
> **Minutes to hours:** asthma, acute pulmonary oedema, anaphylaxis, COPD exacerbation, aspiration.
> **Hours to days:** pneumonia, pleural effusion, decompensating heart failure, anaemia from bleeding.
> **Days to weeks:** malignancy, interstitial lung disease, chronic anaemia, progressive neuromuscular disease.
> Ask "what were you doing when it started, and how long did it take to reach this?" before anything else.

> [!tip] Differential by system, so nothing gets missed
> **Airway:** obstruction, foreign body, anaphylaxis — cross-refer [[A2]].
> **Lung:** pneumonia, PE, pneumothorax, effusion, asthma, COPD, ARDS, haemorrhage.
> **Cardiac:** acute pulmonary oedema, myocardial infarction (dyspnoea may be the anginal equivalent in diabetics, women and the elderly), tamponade, arrhythmia, valvular failure.
> **Metabolic:** any metabolic acidosis — DKA, sepsis, renal failure, salicylate. Cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.1.
> **Haematological:** acute anaemia, carbon monoxide poisoning.
> **Neuromuscular:** Guillain–Barré, myasthenic crisis — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.5.
> **Psychogenic:** a diagnosis of exclusion, never a first move.

> [!danger] Dyspnoea with a clear chest
> Normal auscultation and a normal chest radiograph do **not** reassure. **Pulmonary embolism, metabolic acidosis, anaemia, myocardial ischaemia and early neuromuscular failure all present with breathlessness and clear lungs.** If the chest is clear and the patient is breathless, widen the differential rather than narrowing it.

**S/Smx:** Increased respiratory rate and effort, accessory muscle use, inability to speak in full sentences, positional preference, chest pain, cough, wheeze, stridor, fever, leg swelling, and associated features that point at the system involved.

**Ix:** Oxygen saturation with inspired oxygen concentration recorded (*why:* a saturation without the FiO₂ alongside it cannot be interpreted; *what:* saturation with FiO₂). ABG or VBG with lactate (*why:* separates hypoxaemic from hypercapnic from metabolic causes, which is the first branch of the differential; *what:* PaO₂, PaCO₂, pH, lactate). ECG (*why:* ischaemia, arrhythmia and right heart strain, and dyspnoea may be the only presentation of infarction in diabetics and the elderly; *what:* ischaemic change, arrhythmia, S1Q3T3 or strain pattern). CXR (*why:* the highest-yield single image — consolidation, oedema, pneumothorax, effusion; *what:* findings). Bedside lung ultrasound where available (*why:* faster and more sensitive than radiography at the bedside for pneumothorax, effusion and interstitial oedema, and it does not require moving the patient; *what:* absent lung sliding, B-lines, effusion, consolidation). Bedside echocardiography (*why:* separates cardiogenic from non-cardiogenic and identifies RV strain from PE; *what:* LV function, RV dilatation, pericardial fluid). FBC (*why:* anaemia causes dyspnoea with normal saturation; *what:* haemoglobin). BNP or NT-proBNP where the cardiac-versus-respiratory question is genuinely open (*why:* a normal value makes heart failure unlikely; *what:* elevation — `UNVERIFIED — cut-offs, and the effect of renal impairment, obesity and AF on them`). D-dimer or CTPA per pre-test probability (*why:* PE is the most frequently missed cause of acute dyspnoea; *what:* filling defect). Bedside glucose and ketones (*why:* DKA presents as breathlessness, sometimes with no other respiratory complaint; *what:* hyperglycaemia with ketosis).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
A–E, oxygen to target, sit upright, monitoring, IV access. Treat the identified cause. Decompress a tension pneumothorax on clinical grounds without waiting for imaging.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Cross-refer [[F0.5]] and [[F0.3]].

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Address the underlying condition and its preventer or maintenance therapy.

---

## 0.2 Chronic and Chronic Progressive Dyspnoea

**D:** Breathlessness present over weeks to months, either stable or worsening.

**A/P:** Chronic dyspnoea reflects a persistent limitation in the oxygen delivery chain — ventilation, gas exchange, oxygen carriage, or cardiac output — or an increase in the work required to achieve it. **Deconditioning and obesity are genuine and extremely common contributors** and should be identified rather than assumed once organic causes are addressed.

> [!tip] Cardiac versus respiratory — the discriminating features
> | | Points cardiac | Points respiratory |
> |---|---|---|
> | Orthopnoea, PND | Strongly | Occasionally (COPD, large effusion) |
> | Wheeze | "Cardiac asthma" possible | Common |
> | Sputum | Pink frothy in acute oedema | Chronic, purulent, or large-volume |
> | Ankle oedema | Common | Only with cor pulmonale |
> | Exercise pattern | Fatigue and breathlessness together | Breathlessness with preserved leg strength |
> | Response to diuretic | Improves | Does not |
> The two coexist frequently in older patients, and identifying one does not exclude the other.

> [!warning] Chronic *progressive* dyspnoea deserves a different threshold
> Relentless worsening over months, particularly with a dry cough and clubbing, suggests **interstitial lung disease**. Progressive dyspnoea with a normal chest radiograph and normal spirometry suggests **pulmonary hypertension**, which is characteristically diagnosed late because early investigation is unremarkable. Progressive dyspnoea with weight loss suggests malignancy. Progressive dyspnoea with limb or bulbar weakness suggests neuromuscular disease — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.5.

> [!info] Grading it
> The MRC or modified MRC dyspnoea scale grades functional limitation and is used in COPD assessment and pulmonary rehabilitation referral. `UNVERIFIED — the grade descriptors and the version used in Australian practice require verification.` A practical alternative in any consultation: ask what the patient can no longer do that they could a year ago, and how many stairs or metres they manage now.

> [!tip] Platypnoea-orthodeoxia — the reverse pattern
> Dyspnoea and desaturation **worse when upright and better lying flat**. Rare, and almost always significant — hepatopulmonary syndrome, intracardiac shunt through a patent foramen ovale, or basal arteriovenous malformations. Worth knowing because the pattern is so distinctive that recognising it essentially makes the diagnosis.

**S/Smx:** Exertional breathlessness with declining exercise tolerance, cough, wheeze, chest pain, orthopnoea, ankle swelling, weight loss, fatigue, and on examination clubbing, crackles, wheeze, raised JVP, murmurs, or signs of anaemia.

**Ix:** Spirometry with bronchodilator reversibility (*why:* separates obstructive from restrictive physiology and identifies reversible airflow limitation, and it is the single most useful outpatient test; *what:* FEV1/FVC ratio, reversibility). Full pulmonary function tests with lung volumes and diffusing capacity (*why:* DLCO is reduced in emphysema, ILD and pulmonary vascular disease and preserved in asthma and chest wall disease, which discriminates when spirometry alone is equivocal; *what:* reduced DLCO, restrictive volumes). CXR (*why:* first-line structural assessment; *what:* interstitial change, mass, effusion, cardiomegaly). High-resolution CT chest where ILD is suspected (*why:* the pattern of fibrosis is diagnostic in itself in many cases and CXR is insensitive early; *what:* reticulation, honeycombing, ground glass, distribution). Echocardiography (*why:* assesses systolic and diastolic function, valves, and estimates pulmonary artery pressure — the screening test for pulmonary hypertension; *what:* ejection fraction, diastolic parameters, estimated PA pressure). FBC and iron studies (*why:* anaemia is a common and eminently treatable cause; *what:* haemoglobin, ferritin). TFTs (*why:* both hypo- and hyperthyroidism cause exertional breathlessness; *what:* TSH). BNP (*why:* screening for heart failure in the undifferentiated patient; *what:* elevation). ECG (*why:* ischaemia, hypertrophy, right heart strain; *what:* findings). Six-minute walk test with oximetry (*why:* demonstrates exertional desaturation that resting saturation entirely misses, and quantifies functional capacity objectively; *what:* distance, desaturation on exertion).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Rarely needed unless acutely decompensated.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Respiratory or cardiology referral where the diagnosis is unclear after first-line investigation.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Pulmonary rehabilitation** for chronic respiratory disease — it improves exercise capacity and quality of life more reliably than most drugs and is under-referred. Smoking cessation, weight management, treatment of anaemia, vaccination, and management of breathlessness itself in advanced disease including the palliative use of opioids and handheld fans.

---

## 0.3 Orthopnoea and Paroxysmal Nocturnal Dyspnoea

**D:** Breathlessness on lying flat (orthopnoea), and breathlessness waking the patient from sleep (PND).

**A/P:** Lying flat redistributes blood from the splanchnic circulation and lower limbs into the thorax → increased venous return → raised left atrial and pulmonary capillary pressure → in a failing ventricle that cannot accommodate the extra preload, interstitial pulmonary oedema develops → dyspnoea. Supine posture also raises the diaphragm and reduces functional residual capacity, which is why diaphragmatic weakness, massive ascites and severe obesity produce the same symptom by an entirely mechanical route.
**PND** classically occurs one to two hours after falling asleep — reabsorption of interstitial fluid continues after lying down, and nocturnal reduction in sympathetic tone reduces ventricular support. The patient wakes gasping, sits or stands at a window, and improves over roughly half an hour, which distinguishes it from the near-instantaneous relief of sitting up in orthopnoea.

> [!tip] Quantify it crudely but usefully
> **Ask how many pillows**, and more importantly whether that number has changed. "Three pillows, same as always" is far less informative than "two for years, four for the last fortnight." Ask whether they now sleep in a chair — that is severe orthopnoea and is frequently not volunteered.

> [!warning] Not all orthopnoea is heart failure
> **Large bilateral pleural effusions · massive ascites · severe obesity · bilateral diaphragmatic palsy** (breathless immediately on lying flat, with paradoxical abdominal movement) · **severe COPD** · **large goitre or mediastinal mass.** Nocturnal breathlessness also occurs in asthma (with wheeze and cough, typically in the early hours), obstructive sleep apnoea (with snoring and witnessed apnoeas, and the patient usually settles quickly), and gastro-oesophageal reflux (with acid taste and cough).

> [!tip] Distinguishing nocturnal breathlessness
> | | Timing | Associated | Relief |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | **PND** | 1–2 h after sleep onset | Cough, froth, palpitations | Sitting or standing, over ~30 min |
> | **Asthma** | Early hours | Wheeze, dry cough | Bronchodilator |
> | **OSA** | Repeatedly, any time | Snoring, witnessed apnoea, choking | Settles in seconds |
> | **Reflux** | After late meals, on lying | Acid taste, cough, hoarseness | Sitting, antacid |

**S/Smx:** As above, with the associated features of the underlying cause — raised JVP, third heart sound, basal crackles and peripheral oedema in heart failure.

**Ix:** Echocardiography (*why:* the definitive assessment of the ventricular and valvular function that produces cardiac orthopnoea, and it distinguishes systolic from diastolic failure; *what:* ejection fraction, diastolic parameters, valve lesions). BNP or NT-proBNP (*why:* a normal value substantially reduces the likelihood of heart failure in an undifferentiated patient; *what:* elevation). CXR (*why:* identifies pulmonary congestion, effusions, cardiomegaly and the raised hemidiaphragm of phrenic palsy; *what:* upper lobe diversion, effusions, elevated diaphragm). ECG (*why:* ischaemia, AF and hypertrophy; *what:* findings). Supine and erect spirometry (*why:* a marked fall in FVC on lying flat is characteristic of diaphragmatic weakness and is otherwise easily missed; *what:* postural fall in FVC). Sleep study where OSA is suspected (*why:* confirms and grades, and CPAP is highly effective; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). UEC, FBC, TFTs (*why:* renal failure, anaemia and thyroid disease all contribute; *what:* derangement). Weight and fluid balance charting (*why:* documents the fluid accumulation that drives symptoms and tracks response; *what:* weight trend).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Sit upright, oxygen if hypoxaemic, and manage as acute pulmonary oedema if that is the presentation — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.3.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Diuresis and guideline-directed therapy for heart failure. Drainage of large effusions. CPAP for OSA.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Daily weights with a written action plan, fluid and salt advice, and medication adherence review. A patient who knows to act on a two-kilogram weight gain prevents an admission.

---

## 0.4 Acute Cough

**D:** Cough of less than about three weeks' duration. `UNVERIFIED — the duration boundaries separating acute, subacute and chronic cough require verification.`

**R/Causes:** Viral upper respiratory tract infection is by far the commonest. Also: acute bronchitis, pneumonia, asthma exacerbation, COPD exacerbation, **pertussis**, aspiration, inhaled foreign body, pulmonary embolism, acute pulmonary oedema, and irritant or smoke inhalation.

**A/P:** Irritant receptors in the larynx, trachea and large bronchi are stimulated by inflammation, secretions, foreign material or mechanical distortion → afferent vagal signalling to the brainstem cough centre → the reflex sequence of inspiration, glottic closure, expiratory effort and explosive release.

> [!warning] Pertussis is under-diagnosed in adults and it matters
> **Paroxysms of coughing, post-tussive vomiting, and an inspiratory whoop** — though adults frequently have paroxysms and vomiting without a whoop, and often present as "a cough that won't go away" at several weeks. **Adults and adolescents are the reservoir that infects unimmunised infants**, in whom pertussis causes apnoea and death. It is a **notifiable disease** in Australia, and household contact management including infant prophylaxis matters. `UNVERIFIED — diagnostic method by stage of illness (PCR versus serology), antibiotic regimens, exclusion periods and contact prophylaxis all require verification against eTG and SA Health guidance.`

> [!tip] Post-infectious cough
> A dry cough persisting for weeks after a viral illness resolves is common, benign and self-limiting, caused by transient airway hyperresponsiveness. It needs explanation and reassurance rather than repeated antibiotics — but only once the red flags below are absent.

> [!danger] Red flags in acute cough
> Haemoptysis · breathlessness at rest · pleuritic chest pain · fever with focal chest signs · unilateral wheeze (foreign body) · choking episode · weight loss · immunosuppression · stridor.

**S/Smx:** Cough with or without sputum, coryza, sore throat, fever, wheeze, chest pain, and the paroxysmal pattern of pertussis.

**Ix:** Usually none in an otherwise well patient with a clear viral picture (*why:* investigating uncomplicated viral cough adds cost and radiation without changing management; *what:* not indicated). CXR where red flags are present, focal signs are found, or the patient is systemically unwell (*why:* identifies pneumonia, malignancy, effusion and foreign body complications; *what:* consolidation, mass, collapse). Nasopharyngeal swab for respiratory viral and pertussis PCR (*why:* confirms pertussis, which triggers notification and contact management, and identifies influenza and COVID-19 where antivirals or isolation apply; *what:* pathogen). Oxygen saturation (*why:* separates uncomplicated cough from lower respiratory tract involvement; *what:* hypoxia). Sputum culture where purulent and the patient is unwell or has chronic lung disease (*why:* directs therapy and identifies resistant organisms in bronchiectasis; *what:* organism). Spirometry after resolution where asthma is suspected (*why:* cough may be the sole asthma symptom; *what:* reversible obstruction).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Symptomatic measures. Antibiotics only where bacterial infection is likely — **most acute cough is viral and antibiotics do not shorten it.** Treat asthma or COPD exacerbation where that is the cause.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Notify and manage contacts for pertussis.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Immunisation, including adult pertussis boosters in those around infants — pregnant women and household contacts. Smoking cessation.

---

## 0.5 Chronic Cough

**D:** Cough persisting beyond about eight weeks in an adult. `UNVERIFIED — the duration threshold and the paediatric definition require verification.`

> [!tip] The big three in a non-smoker with a normal chest radiograph
> **Upper airway cough syndrome** (formerly post-nasal drip) — throat clearing, sensation of dripping, cobblestone posterior pharynx.
> **Asthma, including cough-variant asthma** — cough may be the *only* symptom, with no wheeze; nocturnal and exercise-related, and diagnosed by demonstrating variable airflow obstruction or airway hyperresponsiveness.
> **Gastro-oesophageal reflux** — may be entirely "silent", with no heartburn at all, presenting only as cough and hoarseness.
> These three account for the large majority, they frequently coexist, and treatment is often sequential empirical trials.

> [!danger] Check the medication list before anything else
> **ACE inhibitor cough** occurs in a substantial minority, is dry and tickly, and can begin **months to years** after starting the drug — which is why patients and clinicians both fail to connect the two. It resolves after cessation, though this can take weeks. Ceasing the ACE inhibitor is a free diagnostic test and should be done before an extensive workup. An ARB is the usual substitute.

> [!warning] Red flags mandating imaging and referral
> Haemoptysis · weight loss · smoking history, particularly over 40 · persistent hoarseness · dysphagia · recurrent pneumonia in the same location · clubbing · focal chest signs · night sweats and fever (tuberculosis) · immunosuppression · **large-volume daily sputum production** (bronchiectasis) · occupational dust or asbestos exposure.

**R/Causes beyond the big three:** Smoking and chronic bronchitis · bronchiectasis · lung cancer · interstitial lung disease · tuberculosis · non-asthmatic eosinophilic bronchitis · obstructive sleep apnoea · chronic aspiration · habit or tic cough in children · and chronic cough hypersensitivity syndrome, where the reflex itself is sensitised.

**S/Smx:** Character (dry versus productive), timing (nocturnal, on waking, exertional), triggers, associated throat clearing, heartburn, wheeze, and systemic features.

**Ix:** CXR in all cases of genuinely chronic cough (*why:* the baseline test that excludes malignancy, tuberculosis and interstitial disease, and its normality directs you toward the big three; *what:* mass, infiltrate, bronchiectatic change). Spirometry with reversibility (*why:* identifies the asthma that may present as cough alone; *what:* obstruction with reversibility). Bronchial provocation testing where spirometry is normal and asthma is still suspected (*why:* cough-variant asthma has normal baseline spirometry by definition and is otherwise undiagnosable; *what:* airway hyperresponsiveness). Medication review, specifically for ACE inhibitors (*why:* free, instant, and a common answer; *what:* ACE inhibitor on the list). ENT assessment and nasendoscopy (*why:* identifies upper airway cough syndrome and laryngeal pathology; *what:* post-nasal secretions, laryngeal changes). High-resolution CT chest (*why:* the diagnostic test for bronchiectasis and interstitial lung disease, both of which CXR misses; *what:* bronchial dilatation, tree-in-bud, reticulation). Sputum for culture, cytology and mycobacterial studies where indicated (*why:* tuberculosis and non-tuberculous mycobacteria present exactly this way and require specific testing; *what:* acid-fast bacilli, culture). Induced sputum eosinophil count where available (*why:* identifies eosinophilic bronchitis, which responds to inhaled steroids despite normal spirometry; *what:* sputum eosinophilia). Trial of proton pump inhibitor and reflux measures (*why:* a therapeutic trial is often more practical than pH studies; *what:* symptomatic response over weeks).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Rarely required. Stop the ACE inhibitor if present.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Sequential empirical treatment of the big three where the chest radiograph is normal, with each trial given adequate duration before moving on. Specialist referral where red flags are present or treatment fails.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Smoking cessation. Speech pathology for cough suppression therapy in refractory cough hypersensitivity, which has reasonable evidence and is under-used. Airway clearance and specialist follow-up in bronchiectasis.

---

## 0.6 Productive Cough and Sputum Character

**D:** Cough producing sputum, where the volume, character and pattern narrow the differential considerably.

**A/P:** Airway inflammation increases mucus production from goblet cells and submucosal glands, while impaired mucociliary clearance — from smoking, infection, structural damage or ciliary dysfunction — prevents its removal. **The combination produces the retention that both causes cough and predisposes to further infection**, which is the vicious cycle underlying bronchiectasis.

> [!tip] Sputum character and what it suggests
> | Appearance | Suggests |
> |---|---|
> | Clear or white mucoid | Viral, chronic bronchitis, asthma |
> | Yellow or green purulent | Bacterial infection, or simply neutrophils — **purulence alone does not prove bacterial infection** |
> | **Large volume, daily, worse on waking or with posture** | **Bronchiectasis** |
> | Rust-coloured | Classically pneumococcal pneumonia |
> | "Currant jelly" | Classically *Klebsiella* — memorable but unreliable in practice |
> | Foul-smelling, putrid | Anaerobic infection — lung abscess, aspiration |
> | Pink and frothy | Acute pulmonary oedema |
> | Black | Coal dust, smoke inhalation, injecting drug use |
> | Frank blood | See haemoptysis — always investigate |

> [!danger] Chronic daily large-volume sputum is bronchiectasis until proven otherwise
> This is the discriminator most often missed. A patient describing daily sputum production for years, recurrent chest infections, and sputum that is worse in certain positions has bronchiectasis, and **the diagnosis requires HRCT — chest radiography is not sensitive enough.** Once diagnosed, airway clearance, exacerbation plans and a search for an underlying cause (cystic fibrosis, immunodeficiency, allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis, prior severe infection, aspiration) change management substantially.

> [!warning] Haemoptysis is never a symptom to observe
> Distinguish it from haematemesis (dark, acidic, food particles, preceding nausea) and from bleeding from the nose or gums. Causes: bronchiectasis, malignancy, tuberculosis, pneumonia, PE, pulmonary oedema, anticoagulation, vasculitis. **Massive haemoptysis is an emergency** — the risk is asphyxiation rather than exsanguination, so position the patient with the bleeding side **down** to protect the good lung, and involve respiratory, interventional radiology and thoracic surgery urgently.

**S/Smx:** Volume, colour, odour, timing, positional variation, and presence of blood. Associated fever, weight loss, dyspnoea and chest pain.

**Ix:** Sputum culture and sensitivities (*why:* directs therapy, and in bronchiectasis identifies chronic colonisers such as *Pseudomonas* that change both antibiotic choice and prognosis; *what:* organism, sensitivities). Sputum for acid-fast bacilli and mycobacterial culture where indicated (*why:* tuberculosis and non-tuberculous mycobacteria require specific stains and prolonged culture and are missed on routine testing; *what:* AFB, culture). CXR (*why:* first-line for consolidation, abscess, mass; *what:* findings). High-resolution CT chest where bronchiectasis is suspected (*why:* the diagnostic test, and chest radiography is insufficiently sensitive; *what:* bronchial dilatation exceeding the accompanying artery, wall thickening, tree-in-bud). FBC and inflammatory markers (*why:* infection and eosinophilia, the latter suggesting ABPA; *what:* leucocytosis, eosinophilia). Immunoglobulins and, in appropriate patients, sweat test and CFTR genetics (*why:* immunodeficiency and cystic fibrosis are treatable underlying causes of bronchiectasis and are diagnosed in adulthood more often than expected; *what:* low immunoglobulins, positive sweat test). *Aspergillus* IgE and specific IgG where ABPA is considered (*why:* it is a treatable cause of bronchiectasis in asthmatics; *what:* raised total and specific IgE). Bronchoscopy where a localised abnormality, obstruction or unexplained haemoptysis (*why:* visualises and samples endobronchial lesions; *what:* tumour, foreign body, bleeding source).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Treat infection where present. For massive haemoptysis: airway protection, bleeding side down, oxygen, correct coagulopathy, urgent specialist involvement.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Antibiotics guided by culture. Bronchial artery embolisation for massive haemoptysis. Treat the underlying cause of bronchiectasis.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Airway clearance techniques with physiotherapy input** are the mainstay in bronchiectasis and are consistently under-prescribed. Exacerbation action plan, vaccination, exercise, and long-term macrolide therapy in selected patients. Smoking cessation.

---

## 0.7 Solitary Pulmonary Nodule

**D:** A single rounded opacity in the lung, up to approximately 3 cm, surrounded by aerated lung, without associated lymphadenopathy, atelectasis or effusion. `UNVERIFIED — the size boundary defining nodule versus mass requires verification.` A larger lesion is a mass and carries a substantially higher probability of malignancy.

**A/P:** Nodules represent granuloma, hamartoma, infection, primary malignancy, metastasis, vascular malformation or inflammatory lesion. Most are incidental findings on imaging performed for another reason, and the frequency has risen substantially with CT use. **The clinical task is not to diagnose the nodule but to stratify the probability of malignancy and choose the least invasive appropriate follow-up.**

> [!tip] Features favouring benign versus malignant
> **Benign:** small size · smooth well-defined margins · **benign calcification patterns — central, laminated, diffuse, or "popcorn" (characteristic of hamartoma)** · fat density (also hamartoma) · **documented stability over an adequate interval on previous imaging** · younger patient, never-smoker.
> **Malignant:** larger size · **spiculated or lobulated margins** · upper lobe location · part-solid or ground-glass with a solid component · growth on serial imaging · older age, smoking history, previous malignancy, asbestos or radon exposure, family history · associated weight loss or haemoptysis.
> **Eccentric or stippled calcification does not indicate benignity** — malignancy can engulf a pre-existing calcified granuloma.

> [!danger] The highest-yield single action is finding old imaging
> **A nodule that is unchanged over an adequate period on prior films is almost certainly benign, and finding those films can end the workup immediately.** Chase previous chest radiographs and CTs from other hospitals, the GP, and interstate before ordering anything new. This is the step most often skipped and the one that saves the most investigation, cost and anxiety.

> [!warning] Follow-up intervals are guideline-driven and must be read, not recalled
> The **Fleischner Society** recommendations stratify follow-up by nodule size, solid versus subsolid versus ground-glass character, single versus multiple, and patient risk category. `UNVERIFIED — every size threshold, follow-up interval and duration of surveillance is omitted from this file. Obtain the current Fleischner Society guidance and your local radiology department's protocol. These figures are revised periodically, are not intuitive, and reconstructing them from memory is exactly the failure mode this corpus is guarding against.` Note also that the Fleischner criteria do not apply to patients under a certain age, those with known malignancy, or immunocompromised patients, all of whom are managed differently.

> [!info] Australian context
> Granulomatous nodules from prior tuberculosis or non-tuberculous mycobacterial infection occur, and the endemic fungal causes common in North America are not a significant local consideration. Consider prior TB exposure, particularly in patients born overseas. Lung cancer screening programs have implications for how incidental nodules are handled — `UNVERIFIED — confirm the current status and eligibility of the Australian National Lung Cancer Screening Program, which is recent and may have changed.`

**S/Smx:** Usually asymptomatic and incidental. Where symptoms exist — cough, haemoptysis, weight loss, chest pain — the probability of malignancy rises and the lesion is no longer being managed as an incidental nodule.

**Ix:** Retrieval and comparison with all available prior imaging (*why:* documented stability is the fastest route to a benign conclusion and requires no new test; *what:* unchanged size and morphology over an adequate interval). Thin-section CT chest (*why:* characterises margins, density, calcification pattern and precise size far better than a chest radiograph, and the morphology drives risk stratification; *what:* spiculation, calcification pattern, solid versus subsolid, fat density). Risk assessment incorporating age, smoking history, nodule size and location, and prior malignancy (*why:* the follow-up pathway is explicitly risk-stratified and applying the wrong category leads to over- or under-investigation; *what:* estimated malignancy probability). PET-CT for nodules above a threshold size (*why:* metabolic activity supports or reduces suspicion and simultaneously stages the mediastinum, though small nodules and some indolent tumours are falsely negative and infection is falsely positive; *what:* FDG avidity — `UNVERIFIED — the size threshold below which PET is unreliable`). CT-guided percutaneous biopsy or bronchoscopic sampling (*why:* provides tissue where the lesion is accessible and the result will change management; *what:* histology — with pneumothorax a recognised complication of the percutaneous route). Surgical resection, usually video-assisted thoracoscopic wedge resection (*why:* both diagnostic and therapeutic where suspicion is high; *what:* histology and definitive treatment). Sputum cytology, of limited value (*why:* poor sensitivity for peripheral lesions and rarely changes management; *what:* malignant cells if central and large).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
None. This is not an emergency, and communicating that to an understandably anxious patient is part of the job — an incidental nodule is far more often benign than not.

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Surveillance imaging per guideline, tissue sampling, or resection depending on risk category. **Discussion at a lung cancer multidisciplinary meeting** for anything indeterminate or suspicious.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Smoking cessation, which changes the risk of both this nodule and the next one. Completion of the surveillance interval — **nodules lost to follow-up are a recognised source of delayed cancer diagnosis**, so ensure the follow-up is explicitly booked and communicated to the GP in writing rather than left as a recommendation in a radiology report.

---

> Respiratory failure, hypoxia and abnormal respiratory rate → [[A3]] · Acute asthma, COPD, pneumonia, pulmonary oedema, ARDS → [[F0.5]] · Airway obstruction and stridor → [[A2]] · Cardiogenic shock and PE → [[F0.3]] 0.3, 0.5 · Chest pain framework → [[B1]] · Occupational lung disease → [[02_Respiratory]] Pneumoconioses

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Mechanism, risk-assessment structure and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every dose, threshold, reference range and timing figure carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** All antidote dosing, charcoal timing, QRS thresholds and dialysis criteria must come from the **Poisons Information Centre (13 11 26)** and eTG Toxicology.
>
> Toxidromes, paracetamol, opioids, beta-blocker/CCB, toxic alcohols, anaphylaxis and envenomation are built in [[F0.1]] and are cross-referenced rather than repeated. Salicylate is in [[F0.2]] 0.8.

---

## 0.1 The Poisoned Patient — Risk Assessment and General Approach

**D:** The structured approach to any suspected poisoning, deliberate or accidental, occupational or environmental.

**A/P:** Outcome is determined by the interaction of agent, dose, time since exposure and patient factors — not by the drug name alone. **A "large" overdose of a benign agent may need nothing, while a small ingestion of a high-toxicity agent in a child is an emergency.** Risk assessment, not identification, drives every subsequent decision.

> [!tip] The sequence
> **1. Resuscitate** — A–E, glucose, monitoring. Resuscitation precedes identification.
> **2. Risk assess** — agent, dose, formulation (immediate versus slow-release), time since ingestion, co-ingestants, patient weight and comorbidity, and the clinical course so far. This determines everything else.
> **3. Decontaminate** — where indicated and within the useful window.
> **4. Enhance elimination** — urinary alkalinisation, dialysis, repeat-dose charcoal for selected agents.
> **5. Antidote** — where one exists and is indicated.
> **6. Supportive care** — which is what actually saves most poisoned patients.
> **7. Disposition and psychiatric assessment.**

> [!danger] Screening tests in every deliberate self-poisoning
> **Paracetamol level** — common, co-ingested, silent for a day, lethal if missed. Take it regardless of the stated history.
> **ECG** — QRS and QT reveal sodium and potassium channel blockade independent of what the patient says they took.
> **Bedside glucose.**
> Add a **salicylate level** where the acid-base picture is mixed or unexplained.
> The history in deliberate self-poisoning is unreliable in both directions — under- and over-reporting are both common — and these three tests do not depend on it.

> [!warning] Call the Poisons Information Centre — 13 11 26
> This is the expected step in Australian practice, not a fallback. They have access to current toxicological resources, national antivenom and antidote stock locations, and a toxicologist. In an exam, "contact the Poisons Information Centre" is a correct and expected component of the answer.

> [!info] Decontamination and elimination — the principles, not the numbers
> **Activated charcoal** adsorbs many but not all toxins — it does not bind alcohols, metals including iron and lithium, or corrosives. It carries aspiration risk and requires an intact or protected airway. `UNVERIFIED — the time window in which charcoal retains benefit, dosing, and contraindications require verification against eTG Toxicology.`
> **Whole bowel irrigation** is considered for slow-release preparations, body packers and agents charcoal does not bind.
> **Urinary alkalinisation** enhances elimination of weak acids, notably salicylate — cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.8.
> **Haemodialysis** is effective for small, water-soluble, minimally protein-bound toxins with a low volume of distribution — toxic alcohols, salicylate, lithium, metformin. `UNVERIFIED — indications and thresholds require verification and are agent-specific.`
> **Intravenous lipid emulsion** has a role in local anaesthetic systemic toxicity and is used in some refractory lipophilic drug toxicities. `UNVERIFIED — indications and regimen.`

> [!warning] Occupational and chemical exposure
> **Decontaminate before or while treating, with appropriate PPE** — secondary contamination of the treating team is a real and preventable event. Obtain the **Safety Data Sheet** for the substance, which is legally required to be available in Australian workplaces and gives composition and first aid guidance. Consider notification to SafeWork SA and, for some agents, the public health unit. Cross-refer [[P1]] Occupational Exposure and [[F0.1]] 0.3 for organophosphates.

**S/Smx:** Determined by agent. Approach it by toxidrome — cross-refer [[F0.1]] 0.1 for the comparison table.

**Ix:** Paracetamol level, ECG and bedside glucose in every deliberate ingestion, as above (*why:* the history is unreliable and these three detect the treatable and lethal misses; *what:* level against time, QRS and QT, glucose). VBG with electrolytes, anion gap and lactate (*why:* identifies the metabolic acidosis and osmolar-gap pattern that points to specific agents; *what:* HAGMA, lactate, potassium). UEC, LFT, coagulation profile (*why:* baseline organ function, and hepatic and renal impairment alter both toxicity and elimination; *what:* derangement). Serum osmolality where toxic alcohol is plausible (*why:* the gap is raised before the anion gap rises; *what:* osmolar gap — cross-refer [[F0.1]] 0.8). Specific drug levels where they change management — paracetamol, salicylate, lithium, digoxin, iron, carbamazepine, phenytoin, theophylline (*why:* these are the agents where a level alters the decision, whereas a broad "drug screen" rarely does; *what:* level). Urine drug screen only in specific circumstances (*why:* it detects classes rather than quantities, has long detection windows for some agents and misses many, and **rarely changes acute management** — it is over-ordered; *what:* qualitative result, interpreted cautiously). Pregnancy test in women of reproductive age (*why:* alters both fetal risk assessment and some antidote decisions; *what:* positive test).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
A–E, glucose, monitoring, IV access. Call 13 11 26. Screening bloods and ECG. Decontamination where indicated.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Agent-specific antidote, enhanced elimination, and supportive care. Observation period determined by agent and formulation — **slow-release preparations require far longer observation than the patient's current appearance suggests.**

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Mental health assessment before discharge in every deliberate self-poisoning, regardless of how medically trivial the ingestion proved to be.** Means restriction discussion with the patient and family where appropriate, drug and alcohol referral, GP follow-up, and medication supply review. Cross-refer [[N1]] Risk Assessment & Suicidality.

---

## 0.2 Tricyclic Antidepressant Overdose

**D:** Poisoning by tricyclic antidepressants, producing a combined sodium channel blockade, anticholinergic and alpha-blocking syndrome with rapid potential for deterioration.

**R/Causes:** Amitriptyline, nortriptyline, doxepin, clomipramine, imipramine. Still prescribed for neuropathic pain, migraine prophylaxis, insomnia and enuresis as well as depression — **so the tablets are frequently in households where the indication was not psychiatric**, which is worth remembering when assessing availability.

**A/P:** Three simultaneous mechanisms.
**Fast sodium channel blockade** in the myocardium → slowed phase 0 depolarisation → **QRS widening, right axis shift, arrhythmia** and reduced contractility. Acidosis worsens this, since more drug is in the ionised active form.
**Anticholinergic** — the toxidrome in [[F0.1]] 0.2: dry hot skin, mydriasis, ileus, retention, delirium.
**Alpha-1 blockade** → vasodilatation → hypotension, which compounds the myocardial depression.
Additionally, GABA-A antagonism → **seizures**, which cause acidosis, which worsens the sodium channel blockade — a self-reinforcing loop.

> [!danger] TCA overdose deteriorates fast
> A patient can look well and be arresting within an hour. **Anticholinergic features are the distraction; the ECG is the prognostic test.** Any patient with a possible TCA ingestion needs continuous cardiac monitoring and serial ECGs, not a single one at triage.

> [!tip] The ECG findings
> **QRS widening** — the key finding, and its degree correlates with the risk of seizures and ventricular arrhythmia. **A terminal R wave in aVR** with a rightward terminal QRS axis is characteristic of sodium channel blockade. QT prolongation also occurs. `UNVERIFIED — the specific QRS duration thresholds associated with seizure and arrhythmia risk, and the threshold at which bicarbonate is indicated, require verification against eTG Toxicology and the Poisons Information Centre.`

> [!warning] Three things not to do
> **Do not give flumazenil** if a benzodiazepine co-ingestion is suspected — removing benzodiazepine protection in a TCA overdose can precipitate seizures.
> **Do not give class Ia or Ic antiarrhythmics** (procainamide, flecainide) — they are themselves sodium channel blockers and will worsen the toxicity.
> **Do not allow the patient to become acidotic**, including by under-ventilating an intubated patient — acidosis increases the active drug fraction.

**S/Smx:** Anticholinergic features, drowsiness progressing to coma, seizures, hypotension, tachycardia, and arrhythmia.

**Ix:** Serial ECG with continuous monitoring (*why:* QRS width is the prognostic marker and it evolves over hours, so a single normal ECG at presentation does not exclude significant toxicity; *what:* QRS duration, terminal R in aVR, QT, arrhythmia). VBG with pH (*why:* acidosis worsens sodium channel blockade and the pH is a therapeutic target in its own right; *what:* pH, bicarbonate). UEC with potassium (*why:* bicarbonate therapy drives potassium down and hypokalaemia is arrhythmogenic; *what:* potassium, serially). Paracetamol level (*why:* co-ingestion; *what:* level against time). Bedside glucose (*why:* reversible cause of the altered conscious state; *what:* glucose). CK where prolonged seizures or immobility (*why:* rhabdomyolysis; *what:* elevation). TCA levels are **not** useful acutely (*why:* they correlate poorly with clinical severity and turn around too slowly; *what:* not indicated for acute management).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Continuous cardiac monitoring, IV access, airway protection where conscious state is reduced. **Sodium bicarbonate for QRS widening, arrhythmia or hypotension** — it works by both increasing extracellular sodium and alkalinising, reducing the active ionised drug fraction. Benzodiazepines for seizures. Fluid and vasopressor for hypotension. Charcoal in early presentations with a protected airway. Call 13 11 26. `UNVERIFIED — bicarbonate dosing, target pH, QRS threshold and vasopressor choice are all omitted.`

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
ICU admission for significant toxicity. Hyperventilation to maintain alkalosis in the intubated patient. Lipid emulsion and extracorporeal support in refractory cases are toxicologist decisions.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Mental health assessment. **Review of ongoing TCA prescribing** — where an antidepressant is still needed, the lethality of TCAs in overdose is a legitimate consideration in agent choice and quantity dispensed.

---

## 0.3 Benzodiazepine Overdose

**D:** Poisoning by benzodiazepines, usually benign in isolation and dangerous in combination.

**A/P:** Benzodiazepines potentiate GABA-A, increasing the frequency of chloride channel opening → CNS depression. **They do not directly open the channel**, which is why they have a ceiling effect and a wide therapeutic margin in isolation — unlike barbiturates, which do, and which lack that ceiling.

> [!warning] The danger is the combination, not the benzodiazepine
> Isolated benzodiazepine overdose in a healthy adult usually requires only observation and airway monitoring. **The deaths occur with co-ingested opioids, alcohol or other sedatives**, where the respiratory depression is synergistic. Co-prescription of opioids and benzodiazepines is a recognised and avoidable contributor to overdose death, and combined prescribing is now actively discouraged.

> [!danger] Flumazenil is rarely the answer
> It **precipitates seizures** in benzodiazepine-dependent patients and in mixed overdose — particularly with tricyclics, where the benzodiazepine may be the only thing preventing convulsion. It has a shorter duration of action than most benzodiazepines, so re-sedation follows. It is generally **avoided in the undifferentiated overdose patient**, and its main legitimate role is reversal of iatrogenic procedural sedation in a patient known not to be dependent. `UNVERIFIED — indications, dosing and contraindications from eTG and the Poisons Information Centre.`

> [!danger] Benzodiazepine withdrawal is more dangerous than the overdose
> Unlike opioid withdrawal, which is deeply unpleasant but rarely lethal, **benzodiazepine withdrawal causes seizures and delirium and can kill.** Do not abruptly cease benzodiazepines in a dependent inpatient. This is a common inpatient harm when a regular home benzodiazepine is simply omitted from the admission chart.

**S/Smx:** Drowsiness, slurred speech, ataxia, nystagmus, reduced conscious state, respiratory depression when combined with other agents. Pupils are typically normal, which helps distinguish it from opioid toxicity.

**Ix:** Bedside glucose (*why:* mimics and is instantly reversible; *what:* hypoglycaemia). VBG (*why:* identifies the hypercapnia that indicates significant respiratory depression, usually signalling a co-ingestant; *what:* raised pCO₂). Paracetamol level and ECG (*why:* standard deliberate-ingestion screen, and the ECG identifies a co-ingested sodium channel blocker; *what:* level, QRS and QT). Medication chart and community dispensing history (*why:* establishes dependence, which determines both flumazenil safety and the need for a withdrawal plan; *what:* regular benzodiazepine, dose, duration). Pupil examination (*why:* pinpoint pupils redirect toward opioid co-ingestion; *what:* pupil size). CT brain where conscious state does not fit the exposure (*why:* an intracranial event must not be attributed to sedation; *what:* haemorrhage, infarct).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Airway positioning and monitoring, oxygen, supportive care. Ventilatory support if inadequate. Observation until conscious state recovers. **Continue regular benzodiazepines in a dependent patient** rather than omitting them.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Supportive care. Flumazenil only in the narrow circumstances above.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Mental health assessment. Structured, slow tapering plan for dependence, in partnership with the GP — abrupt cessation is unsafe. Check ScriptCheckSA when prescribing. Cross-refer [[F0.4]] 0.9.

---

## 0.4 Carbon Monoxide Poisoning

**D:** Poisoning by carbon monoxide, a colourless odourless gas produced by incomplete combustion.

**R/Causes:** Faulty or unflued gas heaters and water heaters · house fires · petrol generators or engines run in enclosed spaces · vehicle exhaust · charcoal barbecues indoors · **shisha and hookah use** · blocked flues. **Peaks in winter**, and clusters in households.

**A/P:** Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin with an affinity far exceeding oxygen → carboxyhaemoglobin cannot carry oxygen → **and it shifts the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve to the left**, so the remaining oxygen is released to tissues less readily. Both effects reduce delivery. Carbon monoxide additionally binds myoglobin and mitochondrial cytochrome oxidase → direct cellular respiratory impairment and cardiac depression → which is why the tissue injury exceeds what the carboxyhaemoglobin level alone would predict.

> [!danger] Standard pulse oximetry reads normal or high
> Carboxyhaemoglobin absorbs light similarly to oxyhaemoglobin, so **the oximeter cannot distinguish them.** A saturation of 99% in a carbon-monoxide-poisoned patient is meaningless. Diagnosis requires **co-oximetry or a specific carboxyhaemoglobin measurement**, on a venous or arterial sample. Cross-refer [[A3]] 0.6.

> [!warning] The symptoms are entirely non-specific — the history makes the diagnosis
> Headache, nausea, dizziness, fatigue, confusion. Commonly misdiagnosed as viral illness, migraine or gastroenteritis. **The discriminating features are contextual:**
> · **Multiple people in the same household affected**, including pets
> · **Symptoms improve when away from home and return on going back**
> · Winter, with a gas heater in use
> · Symptoms in a group in an enclosed space
> **"Cherry-red" skin is rare, late, and usually a post-mortem finding** — do not rely on it or wait for it.

> [!warning] Pregnancy and children
> **Fetal haemoglobin binds carbon monoxide with higher affinity and clears it more slowly**, so the fetus is at greater risk than the maternal level suggests. The threshold for treatment and for specialist involvement is lower in pregnancy. Children have higher minute ventilation relative to size and accumulate faster.

> [!info] Delayed neuropsychiatric sequelae
> Cognitive impairment, personality change, parkinsonism and memory deficits can develop days to weeks after apparent recovery. **Warn the patient and arrange follow-up** — the absence of symptoms at discharge does not exclude them.

**S/Smx:** Headache (most common), nausea, vomiting, dizziness, confusion, weakness, chest pain, dyspnoea, syncope, seizures, coma. Cardiac ischaemia in those with coronary disease.

**Ix:** Carboxyhaemoglobin level by co-oximetry, venous or arterial (*why:* the diagnostic test, and it is the one that standard oximetry and a routine gas will not give you unless specifically requested; *what:* elevated COHb, interpreted against smoking status and time since removal from exposure, since it falls once on oxygen). VBG or ABG with lactate (*why:* metabolic acidosis and lactate reflect tissue hypoxia severity, and a markedly raised lactate should also prompt thought of concurrent cyanide poisoning in fire victims; *what:* acidosis, lactate). ECG and troponin (*why:* myocardial ischaemia occurs and predicts worse outcome, including in young patients; *what:* ischaemic change, troponin rise). Bedside glucose (*why:* reversible mimic; *what:* glucose). Pregnancy test (*why:* changes the treatment threshold; *what:* positive). CT brain where conscious state is reduced or focal signs present (*why:* excludes alternative pathology and severe cases show basal ganglia changes; *what:* globus pallidus changes, alternative pathology). Cyanide consideration in enclosed-space fire victims (*why:* combustion of synthetic materials releases cyanide and it co-occurs with CO, presenting with profound acidosis out of proportion; *what:* clinical suspicion, high lactate).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
**Remove from the source. High-flow oxygen via a non-rebreather mask immediately** — this substantially shortens the carboxyhaemoglobin half-life compared with room air, and is the single most important intervention. Continue oxygen regardless of the pulse oximeter reading. Monitoring, IV access, ECG. Call 13 11 26.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
**Hyperbaric oxygen** is considered for severe poisoning — loss of consciousness, neurological signs, cardiac ischaemia, pregnancy, or a very high level — though the evidence for its benefit over normobaric oxygen remains debated and access in South Australia requires discussion with the hyperbaric service. `UNVERIFIED — indications, carboxyhaemoglobin thresholds, oxygen half-life figures, and the location and referral pathway for hyperbaric facilities require verification with the Poisons Information Centre.`

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Source identification and rectification before the patient returns home** — this is the part that prevents the next case, and it may require the gas supplier or an appliance inspection. Warn about delayed neuropsychiatric sequelae and arrange follow-up. Consider whether other household members need assessment.

---

## 0.5 Adverse Drug Reaction

**D:** A harmful or unintended response to a medicine at doses used for prophylaxis, diagnosis or therapy.

> [!tip] Classification — Type A versus Type B is the useful first split
> **Type A ("Augmented")** — an exaggeration of the drug's known pharmacology. **Dose-related, predictable, common**, and managed by dose reduction. Bleeding on warfarin, bradycardia on a beta-blocker, hypoglycaemia on insulin, constipation on opioids.
> **Type B ("Bizarre")** — idiosyncratic, **not dose-related, unpredictable**, often immunological. Uncommon but disproportionately severe. Anaphylaxis, SJS/TEN, drug-induced liver injury, agranulocytosis. Managed by **stopping the drug and never rechallenging.**
> Extended categories: **C** chronic (osteoporosis on long-term steroids), **D** delayed (malignancy, teratogenesis), **E** end-of-use withdrawal (benzodiazepine withdrawal, steroid crisis, beta-blocker rebound), **F** failure of therapy.

> [!danger] Distinguish true allergy from intolerance — the penicillin problem
> A large proportion of patients labelled "penicillin allergic" are not. Most report gastrointestinal upset, a non-specific childhood rash, or an event they cannot describe. **The label carries real harm** — it drives use of broader-spectrum, less effective and more toxic alternatives, with worse outcomes for surgical prophylaxis and serious infection, and it increases resistant organism acquisition.
> **Take an allergy history properly:** what exactly happened, how soon after the dose, what treatment was needed, and have they tolerated any related antibiotic since. Features suggesting true IgE-mediated allergy are onset within an hour, urticaria, angioedema, wheeze, hypotension. Features suggesting it was not are isolated nausea, a delayed non-urticarial rash in a child who was also viraemic, or an event decades ago with no detail.
> **Correcting an inaccurate allergy label is a genuinely valuable clinical act.** Refer to immunology for formal assessment and de-labelling where it matters. `UNVERIFIED — confirm current Australian de-labelling pathways and referral criteria.`

> [!warning] Reporting
> Adverse events are reported to the **TGA**, which maintains the Database of Adverse Event Notifications. Newly registered medicines carry a **Black Triangle** indicating they are subject to additional monitoring, and reporting for these is particularly encouraged. `UNVERIFIED — confirm current TGA reporting mechanisms and what must be reported.`

**R:**
*Unmodifiable:* Age at both extremes, genetic polymorphisms in drug metabolism (CYP2D6, HLA types), female sex for some reactions, renal and hepatic impairment.
*Modifiable:* **Polypharmacy** — risk rises steeply with the number of medicines · prescribing errors · failure to adjust for renal function · drug interactions · inadequate monitoring · unclear medication reconciliation at transitions of care.

**A/P:** Type A reactions follow directly from the drug's mechanism acting more strongly than intended, usually because of accumulation (renal or hepatic impairment), an interaction (enzyme inhibition), or a dosing error. Type B reactions typically involve immune recognition — IgE-mediated, T-cell mediated, or through drug binding to HLA molecules — or an idiosyncratic metabolic susceptibility.

**S/Smx:** Any organ system. The clue is temporal — a new symptom following a new drug, or following a dose increase, or following the addition of an interacting agent.

**Ix:** **Full medication reconciliation including over-the-counter, complementary and recently ceased medicines** (*why:* the answer is usually in the list, and complementary medicines interact more than patients expect — St John's wort being a notable enzyme inducer; *what:* temporal relationship between drug and symptom). UEC and eGFR (*why:* renal impairment is the commonest reason a Type A reaction develops in a patient who tolerated the drug previously; *what:* declining eGFR requiring dose adjustment). LFT (*why:* drug-induced liver injury is a leading cause of acute liver failure and is frequently drug-related before it is anything else; *what:* hepatocellular or cholestatic pattern). FBC (*why:* agranulocytosis, aplasia and eosinophilia; *what:* neutropenia, eosinophilia). Drug levels for narrow therapeutic index agents — digoxin, lithium, phenytoin, gentamicin, vancomycin (*why:* confirms accumulation as the mechanism; *what:* supratherapeutic level). Interaction check against a reference (*why:* enzyme inhibition and induction explain a large proportion of Type A reactions and are systematically under-checked; *what:* interacting pair). Rechallenge is **not** an investigation for Type B reactions (*why:* it risks a more severe reaction; *what:* contraindicated).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Stop or reduce the suspected agent. Treat the reaction. For severe Type B reactions — anaphylaxis, SCARs, agranulocytosis — cease immediately and permanently.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Substitute with an agent of a different class where continued treatment is needed. Immunology referral for assessment where the label matters clinically.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Document the reaction accurately in the record and the discharge summary — what the drug was, what happened, and how soon.** A documented "allergy: penicillin" with no detail is nearly useless and propagates for decades. Medication alert, patient education, and deprescribing review. Cross-refer [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]] Polypharmacy and Deprescribing.

---

## 0.6 Severe Cutaneous Adverse Reactions

**D:** The subset of Type B reactions with skin involvement and systemic severity — Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis, DRESS, and acute generalised exanthematous pustulosis.

**A/P:** Drug or its metabolite binds HLA molecules and is presented to T cells → cytotoxic T-cell mediated keratinocyte apoptosis (SJS/TEN) or a delayed multi-organ hypersensitivity with eosinophil recruitment and viral reactivation (DRESS). **The HLA association explains why some reactions cluster in specific ancestral populations** and why pre-emptive genotyping is used for certain drugs.

> [!tip] The three, and how to tell them apart
> | | Latency | Skin | Systemic | Key marker |
> |---|---|---|---|---|
> | **SJS / TEN** | ~4 days–4 weeks | Painful skin, dusky macules, blisters, **positive Nikolsky sign**, epidermal detachment | Fever, mucositis | **Mucosal involvement at two or more sites** — eyes, mouth, genitals |
> | **DRESS** | **2–8 weeks — long latency** | Widespread morbilliform rash, **facial oedema** | Fever, lymphadenopathy, hepatitis, nephritis, myocarditis | **Eosinophilia**, deranged LFTs |
> | **AGEP** | Days, often < 48 h | Numerous small sterile **pustules** on erythema | Fever | Neutrophilia, usually self-limiting |
> SJS and TEN are the same disease separated by the percentage of body surface with epidermal detachment. `UNVERIFIED — the body surface area thresholds separating SJS, SJS/TEN overlap and TEN require verification.`

> [!danger] Common culprits worth knowing
> **Allopurinol · anticonvulsants — carbamazepine, lamotrigine, phenytoin, phenobarbitone · sulfonamides including trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole · NSAIDs · beta-lactams · nevirapine.** The long latency of DRESS is the reason it is missed — the drug started six weeks ago is not the one people suspect.
> **HLA-B\*5701 and abacavir**, **HLA-B\*5801 and allopurinol**, and **HLA-B\*1502 and carbamazepine** in some Asian populations are established associations, with pre-emptive testing recommended in specific circumstances. `UNVERIFIED — confirm which HLA tests are recommended and funded in Australian practice and for which populations.`

> [!warning] Mucosal involvement changes everything
> A morbilliform drug rash is common and usually benign. **Painful skin, blistering, mucosal ulceration, facial oedema, fever or systemic upset moves it into the severe category** and requires immediate drug cessation and specialist involvement. These are the features to ask about and look for in any drug rash.

**S/Smx:** As per the table. Prodromal fever and malaise often precede the rash. TEN patients are systemically unwell with extensive skin loss and behave physiologically like burns patients.

**Ix:** FBC with differential (*why:* eosinophilia supports DRESS, neutrophilia supports AGEP, and both are simple and immediately available; *what:* eosinophil count, neutrophils). LFT and UEC (*why:* DRESS causes hepatitis and nephritis and organ involvement determines severity and steroid need; *what:* transaminitis, rising creatinine). Skin biopsy (*why:* distinguishes the entities histologically where clinical features overlap; *what:* full-thickness epidermal necrosis in TEN, spongiosis and eosinophils in DRESS). Blood cultures and infection screen (*why:* sepsis is the principal cause of death in TEN, and infection is also the main differential for a febrile rash; *what:* organism). Full drug timeline including drugs started in the preceding two months (*why:* DRESS latency is long and the culprit is routinely overlooked because it was started weeks earlier; *what:* temporal correlation). Viral serology and PCR for HHV-6, EBV and CMV in DRESS (*why:* reactivation is characteristic and supports the diagnosis; *what:* reactivation). Ophthalmology assessment in SJS/TEN (*why:* ocular involvement causes permanent scarring and vision loss if not treated early; *what:* conjunctival involvement).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
**Cease the culprit drug and all non-essential medications immediately.** Fluid resuscitation, analgesia, temperature control, and meticulous skin and wound care. Involve dermatology urgently, and **manage extensive TEN in a burns unit** — the physiology is that of a major burn.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Supportive care is the mainstay in TEN. Systemic corticosteroids are used in DRESS, often with a prolonged taper because relapse on rapid withdrawal is common. Ophthalmology, urology and gynaecology input for mucosal involvement.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Absolute lifelong avoidance of the culprit drug and its class**, documented prominently with a medical alert. Family counselling where an HLA association exists, since relatives may share the allele. Long-term sequelae — ocular scarring, strictures, chronic skin changes — need follow-up. Cross-refer [[09_01_Dermatology_-_Dermatological_Emergencies]].

---

## 0.7 Immunotherapy Toxicity

**D:** Immune-related adverse events caused by immune checkpoint inhibitors, in which removal of immune self-tolerance produces autoimmune inflammation in any organ.

**R/Causes:** Anti-PD-1 (pembrolizumab, nivolumab), anti-PD-L1 (atezolizumab, durvalumab) and anti-CTLA-4 (ipilimumab) agents, used across an expanding range of malignancies. **Combination therapy carries substantially higher toxicity than single agents.** Pre-existing autoimmune disease increases risk.

**A/P:** Checkpoint pathways normally restrain T-cell activation to preserve self-tolerance. Blocking them unleashes anti-tumour immunity — and, by the same mechanism, **autoimmunity against normal tissue.** The toxicity is therefore not a drug side effect in the conventional sense but an intended mechanism acting in the wrong place, which is why the treatment is immunosuppression rather than antidote or supportive care alone.

> [!danger] The single most important clinical point
> **A patient on checkpoint inhibitor therapy presenting with almost any new symptom has an immune-related adverse event until proven otherwise — and the treatment is corticosteroids, not antibiotics.**
> Diarrhoea is colitis, not gastroenteritis. Breathlessness is pneumonitis, not infection. Fatigue is hypophysitis or adrenal insufficiency, not deconditioning. Infection must still be excluded, but the reflex to treat as infection and discharge is the recognised error, and it causes deaths.
> **Ask every oncology patient what they are on**, flag it, and **contact their oncology team** — most Australian cancer centres run a 24-hour line for exactly this.

> [!warning] Timing does not exclude it
> Immune-related adverse events occur at any point — within weeks of the first dose, months in, or **after treatment has stopped**, sometimes by many months. "They finished immunotherapy last year" does not exclude the diagnosis.

> [!tip] By organ, with the ones that must not be missed
> **Colitis** — diarrhoea, abdominal pain; risk of perforation. Common with anti-CTLA-4.
> **Hepatitis** — usually asymptomatic transaminitis found on bloods.
> **Pneumonitis** — dry cough, dyspnoea, ground-glass change on CT; potentially fatal.
> **Thyroiditis** — thyrotoxic phase followed by hypothyroidism, often permanent.
> **Hypophysitis and adrenal insufficiency** — fatigue, headache, hypotension, hyponatraemia. **May present as adrenal crisis** — cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.6.
> **Myocarditis** — rare but with **high mortality**; chest pain, dyspnoea, arrhythmia, raised troponin. Any cardiac symptom in a patient on immunotherapy warrants troponin and ECG.
> **Dermatological** — rash and pruritus commonly, but SCARs occur — see 0.6.
> **Neurological** — myasthenia-like syndrome, Guillain–Barré-like presentations, encephalitis.

**S/Smx:** Organ-specific as above. Frequently non-specific early — fatigue, low-grade fever, malaise — which is precisely why the exposure history is the key to the diagnosis.

**Ix:** Full blood count, UEC, LFT, TFTs, glucose and cortisol (*why:* this panel screens most of the common endocrine, hepatic and renal irAEs simultaneously, and several are asymptomatic when first detectable; *what:* transaminitis, deranged TFTs, hyponatraemia, low cortisol, new hyperglycaemia from immune-mediated diabetes). Troponin and ECG for any cardiac symptom (*why:* myocarditis has high mortality and is treatable if recognised early; *what:* troponin rise, conduction abnormality). CT chest where respiratory symptoms (*why:* pneumonitis is not distinguishable clinically from infection and CXR is insensitive; *what:* ground-glass opacity, organising pneumonia pattern). Stool culture, *C. difficile* toxin and PCR panel (*why:* infection must be excluded before immunosuppressing a patient with diarrhoea, and the two can coexist; *what:* pathogen). Flexible sigmoidoscopy with biopsy in significant colitis (*why:* confirms immune colitis and grades severity, guiding steroid and escalation decisions; *what:* colitis, ulceration). ACTH and paired cortisol (*why:* distinguishes primary adrenal insufficiency from hypophysitis, which have different management; *what:* ACTH level). Baseline and serial monitoring bloods per the oncology protocol (*why:* many irAEs are detected on routine bloods before symptoms; *what:* trend).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
Assess severity by grade. **Withhold the immunotherapy. Exclude infection, then start corticosteroids** for anything beyond mild. **Contact the treating oncology service.** For suspected myocarditis or adrenal crisis, treat as an emergency. `UNVERIFIED — corticosteroid doses by toxicity grade, the grading criteria themselves, and escalation thresholds require verification against the treating centre's protocol and current oncology guidance.`

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Corticosteroids with a slow taper — **rapid tapering causes relapse** and courses are often measured in weeks. Escalation to infliximab, mycophenolate or other agents in steroid-refractory cases, directed by oncology. Decision on rechallenge is a specialist one and depends on the organ involved and severity.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Endocrinopathies are frequently permanent** and require lifelong replacement — thyroid hormone, hydrocortisone, insulin. Patients on long-term steroids need PJP prophylaxis, bone protection and glycaemic monitoring. Ensure the patient carries documentation of their immunotherapy exposure, because the next clinician to see them may not think of it.

---
