> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Mechanism, classification and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every temperature threshold, voltage cut-off, dose, cooling rate and observation period carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against ANZCOR (drowning, hypothermia, electrical injury), eTG, and SA Health heatwave guidance.

---

## 0.1 Heat Illness — The Spectrum

**D:** A continuum of disorders caused by heat gain exceeding heat loss, ranging from cramps through heat exhaustion to heat stroke.

**A/P:** Heat is dissipated by radiation, convection, conduction and **evaporation of sweat**, the last being the dominant route in a hot environment. Rising ambient temperature abolishes the gradient for radiation and convection → evaporation becomes the only route → **high humidity then abolishes evaporation as well**, which is why humid heat is far more dangerous than dry heat at the same temperature. Compensatory cutaneous vasodilatation diverts blood from the splanchnic and renal beds and, combined with sweat losses, produces volume depletion. When compensation fails, core temperature rises → direct cellular thermal injury and a systemic inflammatory response → multi-organ failure.

> [!tip] Where the line sits
> | | Core temperature | Mental state | Sweating | Severity |
> |---|---|---|---|---|
> | **Heat cramps** | Normal or mildly raised | Normal | Present | Minor |
> | **Heat exhaustion** | Raised but below the heat stroke threshold | **Normal — this is the discriminator** | Usually present | Serious, reversible |
> | **Heat stroke** | Markedly raised | **Altered — confusion, ataxia, seizure, coma** | May be absent (classic) or present (exertional) | Life-threatening |
> `UNVERIFIED — the specific core temperature thresholds defining each stage require verification; no values are stated here.`

> [!danger] Central nervous system dysfunction is the discriminator, not the thermometer
> **Any altered mental state in a hyperthermic patient is heat stroke until proven otherwise**, regardless of the recorded temperature — which may have already fallen with initial cooling or been measured peripherally and underestimated. Do not require a threshold number before treating aggressively.
> **Sweating does not exclude heat stroke.** The teaching that heat stroke patients are dry applies mainly to the classic non-exertional form; exertional heat stroke patients are frequently drenched.

> [!warning] Australian context
> **Heatwaves cause more deaths in Australia than any other natural hazard.** The at-risk groups are the elderly, the socially isolated, those on anticholinergics, antipsychotics, diuretics and beta-blockers, people with chronic disease, infants, and outdoor workers. South Australia has heatwave warning systems and health service escalation plans. Asking about air conditioning, fan access, fluid intake and whether anyone is checking on the person is a legitimate part of the assessment, particularly in Adelaide summers.

> [!tip] Classic versus exertional heat stroke
> **Classic (non-exertional):** elderly, chronically unwell, during a heatwave, developing over days, often anhidrotic, frequently found at home. Higher mortality.
> **Exertional:** young, fit, athletes, military recruits, labourers, developing over hours, sweating often preserved, higher rates of rhabdomyolysis. Responds well to rapid cooling.

**S/Smx:** Cramps, fatigue, headache, nausea, dizziness, syncope, tachycardia, hypotension and heavy sweating in heat exhaustion. In heat stroke: confusion, irritability, ataxia, slurred speech, seizures, coma, and signs of multi-organ dysfunction.

**Ix:** Core temperature, measured centrally (*why:* oral, axillary and tympanic readings systematically underestimate core temperature and can falsely reassure — rectal or oesophageal measurement is required for an accurate value and for tracking the cooling response; *what:* core temperature and its trend). UEC with potassium (*why:* rhabdomyolysis produces hyperkalaemia and acute kidney injury, and sodium abnormalities occur in both directions depending on what was drunk; *what:* creatinine, potassium, sodium). **CK** (*why:* rhabdomyolysis is common in exertional heat stroke and drives the renal failure; *what:* marked elevation). VBG with lactate (*why:* quantifies hypoperfusion and metabolic derangement; *what:* acidosis, raised lactate). Bedside glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia occurs and mimics the neurological picture; *what:* low glucose). Coagulation profile (*why:* DIC is a feature of severe heat stroke and predicts poor outcome; *what:* prolonged INR, low fibrinogen, thrombocytopenia). LFT (*why:* hepatic injury is characteristic and peaks 24–72 hours after the event, so normal early results do not exclude it; *what:* transaminitis, delayed). ECG (*why:* electrolyte effects and ischaemia; *what:* arrhythmia, hyperkalaemic changes). Urinalysis (*why:* myoglobinuria shows as blood on dipstick without red cells on microscopy — a useful and cheap clue; *what:* dipstick-positive blood with no RBCs). Septic screen where the picture is ambiguous (*why:* sepsis and heat stroke are hard to separate clinically and both need treating; *what:* source).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
See 0.2 for heat stroke. For heat exhaustion: move to a cool environment, remove excess clothing, oral or intravenous rehydration, and rest. Most recover rapidly, and failure to improve should prompt reassessment for heat stroke.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Correct electrolyte and volume deficits. Identify and address the precipitating circumstances.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Prevention advice — hydration, avoiding exertion in peak heat, acclimatisation, and checking on isolated elderly relatives and neighbours during heat events. Medication review, since anticholinergics, antipsychotics and diuretics all impair thermoregulation.

---

## 0.2 Heat Stroke and Severe Hyperthermia

**D:** Core hyperthermia with central nervous system dysfunction and systemic inflammatory response, progressing to multi-organ failure.

**A/P:** Sustained cellular temperature above the tolerance threshold → direct protein denaturation and membrane injury → cytokine release and endothelial activation → a systemic inflammatory response indistinguishable physiologically from sepsis → **DIC, rhabdomyolysis, acute kidney injury, hepatic necrosis, ARDS and cerebral oedema.** Injury accumulates as a function of both temperature and **time spent above the threshold**, which is the entire rationale for speed of cooling.

> [!danger] Time to cooling determines outcome
> **Cool first, investigate second.** The single most important determinant of survival and neurological outcome is how quickly core temperature is brought down. Do not delay cooling for imaging, cannulation or a full history.
> **Cold water immersion is the most effective method** for exertional heat stroke where feasible. Evaporative cooling with tepid water spray and fans, ice packs to the groin, axillae and neck, and cold intravenous fluids are alternatives, particularly in the elderly or where immersion is impractical. `UNVERIFIED — the target core temperature at which active cooling is stopped, the recommended cooling rate, and the preferred method by patient group require verification against eTG and ANZCOR.`

> [!warning] Antipyretics do not work, and dantrolene is not indicated
> **Paracetamol and NSAIDs are useless in heat stroke** — the hypothalamic set point is normal, and this is a failure of heat dissipation, not a raised thermostat. NSAIDs additionally worsen the renal injury and the coagulopathy. **Dantrolene has no established role in heat stroke**, though it is the specific treatment for malignant hyperthermia and is used in neuroleptic malignant syndrome — see 0.3.

> [!warning] Shivering during cooling
> Aggressive cooling induces shivering, which generates heat and defeats the intervention. Benzodiazepines are used to suppress it. `UNVERIFIED — agent and dosing.`

**S/Smx:** Hyperthermia with confusion, agitation, ataxia, seizures or coma. Tachycardia, hypotension, tachypnoea. Skin hot, dry or sweating depending on subtype. Later: oliguria, bleeding, jaundice.

**Ix:** As per 0.1, with serial monitoring. The hepatic injury deserves specific mention (*why:* transaminases peak at 24–72 hours, so a normal LFT on arrival is expected and does not exclude significant injury — repeat them; *what:* delayed transaminase rise). Serial CK and renal function (*why:* rhabdomyolysis evolves over the first days and drives the renal failure; *what:* CK trajectory, creatinine).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
**Start cooling immediately.** A–E, airway protection if conscious state is reduced, oxygen, IV access, fluid resuscitation with careful reassessment. Benzodiazepines for seizures and shivering. Bedside glucose. Continuous core temperature monitoring. ICU involvement.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
ICU management of organ failure. Renal replacement therapy for severe rhabdomyolysis-associated acute kidney injury. Blood product support for DIC. Ventilation for ARDS. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] for the shock physiology.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Some patients have persistent neurological deficit and heat intolerance. Return-to-work and return-to-sport advice with acclimatisation. Consider malignant hyperthermia susceptibility testing where the presentation was exertional and unusual.

---

## 0.3 Hyperthermia versus Fever, and the Drug-Induced Hyperthermias

**D:** Elevated body temperature from failure of heat dissipation or excess heat production, as distinct from fever, which is a regulated rise in the hypothalamic set point.

> [!tip] The distinction that changes management
> **Fever** — the hypothalamic set point is *raised* by pyrogens. The body actively defends the higher temperature: the patient feels cold, shivers, and vasoconstricts. **Antipyretics work**, because they lower the set point.
> **Hyperthermia** — the set point is *normal* and the body is failing to keep up. The patient feels hot and attempts to dissipate. **Antipyretics do not work.** The treatment is physical cooling and removal of the cause.
> Getting this wrong means giving paracetamol to a heat stroke or serotonin toxicity patient and waiting for a response that will not come.

> [!info] The drug-induced hyperthermic syndromes — the discriminating table
> | | Trigger | Onset | Tone | Reflexes | Specific treatment |
> |---|---|---|---|---|---|
> | **Malignant hyperthermia** | Volatile anaesthetics, suxamethonium | Minutes–hours, intra-operative | **Generalised rigidity, masseter spasm** | Normal | **Dantrolene** |
> | **Neuroleptic malignant syndrome** | Antipsychotics; also abrupt dopamine agonist withdrawal | **Days to weeks** | **Lead-pipe rigidity, generalised** | Reduced or normal | Cease agent, supportive; dantrolene and bromocriptine used |
> | **Serotonin toxicity** | Serotonergic combination | **Hours** | Rigidity, **legs > arms** | **Hyperreflexia, clonus** | Cease agents, benzodiazepines, cyproheptadine |
> | **Anticholinergic** | Antimuscarinics | Hours | Normal | Normal | Supportive, cooling |
> | **Sympathomimetic** | Stimulants | Minutes–hours | Normal | Normal | Benzodiazepines, cooling |
> | **Thyroid storm** | Thyrotoxicosis + precipitant | Hours–days | Normal | Brisk | Antithyroid therapy, beta-blockade |
> Serotonin toxicity and sympathomimetic toxidrome are covered in full in [[F0.1]] 0.4.

> [!danger] Malignant hyperthermia — the earliest sign is not the temperature
> **A rising end-tidal CO₂ that does not respond to increased minute ventilation is the earliest and most sensitive sign**, appearing before temperature rises. Masseter spasm on induction after suxamethonium is a classic early clue. It is an autosomal dominant susceptibility, usually involving the ryanodine receptor, so **family history matters and relatives require counselling and testing.** Dantrolene must be immediately available wherever triggering agents are used. `UNVERIFIED — dantrolene dosing and reconstitution requirements from the ANZCA crisis manual and local protocol.`

**S/Smx:** Hyperthermia with the syndrome-specific features above. In all: tachycardia, tachypnoea, autonomic instability, and risk of rhabdomyolysis, DIC and multi-organ failure.

**Ix:** Core temperature (*why:* accurate measurement and trend; *what:* core value). CK (*why:* rhabdomyolysis is prominent in malignant hyperthermia and NMS and drives renal failure; *what:* marked elevation). VBG with end-tidal CO₂ where ventilated (*why:* the rising CO₂ of malignant hyperthermia is the earliest detectable abnormality; *what:* rising ETCO₂, metabolic acidosis). UEC with potassium (*why:* hyperkalaemia from muscle breakdown is immediately life-threatening; *what:* potassium, creatinine). Coagulation profile (*why:* DIC; *what:* deranged INR, low fibrinogen). Medication and anaesthetic chart review with a full timeline (*why:* the syndrome is identified by what was given and when — days for NMS, hours for serotonin toxicity, minutes for malignant hyperthermia; *what:* agent and interval). Neurological examination for clonus and tone (*why:* the tone and reflex pattern is the primary bedside discriminator between the syndromes; *what:* clonus and hyperreflexia versus lead-pipe rigidity). TFTs where thyroid storm is possible (*why:* it is treatable and easily overlooked in an agitated hyperthermic patient; *what:* suppressed TSH, raised free T4).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Cease the causative agent. Physical cooling. Benzodiazepines for agitation, rigidity and shivering. Fluid resuscitation. **Dantrolene immediately for malignant hyperthermia.** Call 13 11 26. Avoid antipsychotics for agitation in these syndromes.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Syndrome-specific as per the table. ICU for severe cases.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Document the trigger permanently and prominently** — malignant hyperthermia susceptibility must be flagged for all future anaesthesia, and family screening arranged. Medication alert for NMS and serotonin toxicity, with the interacting pair documented explicitly.

---

## 0.4 Heat Intolerance

**D:** Subjective inability to tolerate warm environments, as a chronic symptom rather than an acute emergency.

**R/Causes:**
**Endocrine:** thyrotoxicosis (the classic answer) · menopause and perimenopause · phaeochromocytoma · carcinoid.
**Neurological:** **multiple sclerosis — Uhthoff phenomenon**, where a small rise in core temperature transiently worsens existing neurological deficits, characteristically visual, after a hot shower or exercise · autonomic neuropathy · spinal cord injury above the mid-thoracic level, which impairs sweating and vasomotor control below the lesion.
**Pharmacological:** anticholinergics, antipsychotics, beta-blockers, diuretics, topiramate — all impair sweating or thermoregulation.
**Other:** obesity and deconditioning · anhidrosis from ectodermal dysplasia or extensive burn scarring · anxiety · pregnancy · anaemia.

**A/P:** Heat tolerance depends on cardiac output reserve, cutaneous vasodilatation and sweat production. Impairment of any one — by drugs, autonomic dysfunction, skin disease or a hypermetabolic state generating excess heat — reduces the margin, and the patient becomes symptomatic at ambient temperatures others tolerate.

> [!tip] Uhthoff phenomenon is worth recognising
> Transient worsening of MS symptoms with a rise in body temperature — hot shower, exercise, fever — that **resolves on cooling.** It represents conduction block in already demyelinated axons rather than new demyelination, so it is **not a relapse** and does not warrant steroids. Misinterpreting it as a relapse leads to unnecessary treatment.

> [!warning] Heat intolerance with weight loss
> Thyrotoxicosis until proven otherwise — with palpitations, tremor, anxiety, diarrhoea and proximal weakness. Cross-refer [[I1]] Thyroid.

**S/Smx:** Discomfort, sweating, flushing, fatigue or symptom exacerbation in warm conditions. Associated features direct the differential — weight loss and palpitations toward thyroid, episodic headache and hypertension toward phaeochromocytoma, neurological worsening toward MS.

**Ix:** TFTs (*why:* thyrotoxicosis is the commonest treatable endocrine cause and the test is simple; *what:* suppressed TSH, raised free T4 and T3). FBC and iron studies (*why:* anaemia reduces heat tolerance and is easily missed; *what:* haemoglobin, ferritin). Medication review (*why:* anticholinergic and antipsychotic burden is a common and reversible cause, particularly in the elderly; *what:* offending agents). Neurological examination and MRI where MS is suspected (*why:* Uhthoff phenomenon may be the presenting complaint of undiagnosed MS; *what:* demyelinating lesions). Plasma or urinary metanephrines where episodic symptoms with hypertension (*why:* phaeochromocytoma is rare, dangerous and diagnosable; *what:* raised metanephrines). Autonomic function testing in selected patients (*why:* characterises autonomic neuropathy; *what:* abnormal responses). Menopausal history and FSH where age-appropriate (*why:* vasomotor symptoms are extremely common and treatable; *what:* clinical picture — cross-refer [[O4]] Menopausal Symptom).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the underlying cause.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Cooling strategies, air conditioning access, activity timing, and cooling garments in MS. Medication rationalisation. **In patients with impaired thermoregulation, an explicit heatwave plan** — this group is disproportionately represented in heat-related deaths.

---

## 0.5 Hypothermia

**D:** Core body temperature below the normal range, graded mild, moderate and severe. `UNVERIFIED — the temperature boundaries defining each grade require verification against ANZCOR; no values are stated here.`

**R/Causes:** Environmental exposure · **immobility after a fall, particularly an elderly person on the floor overnight — the commonest Australian presentation, and it occurs indoors** · alcohol and drug intoxication, which impairs both behaviour and vasoconstriction · hypoglycaemia · hypothyroidism and myxoedema · adrenal insufficiency · sepsis, which causes hypothermia rather than fever in the elderly and is a poor prognostic sign · burns and extensive skin disease · trauma with cold exposure and cold fluid resuscitation · homelessness.

**A/P:** Heat loss exceeds production → shivering thermogenesis and vasoconstriction initially compensate → **below a threshold, shivering ceases**, removing the main endogenous heat source → progressive slowing of all enzymatic and cardiac function → bradycardia, reduced conscious state, and **myocardial irritability with a marked susceptibility to ventricular fibrillation on movement.** Cold also causes a diuresis (cold diuresis, from central volume redistribution), coagulopathy independent of clotting factor levels, and a left shift of the oxyhaemoglobin curve.

> [!danger] "Not dead until warm and dead"
> Hypothermia is profoundly neuroprotective. **Patients have survived neurologically intact after prolonged arrest in severe hypothermia, including with very long CPR times.** Resuscitation should be continued and the patient rewarmed before death is declared, and extracorporeal rewarming is the definitive approach in hypothermic arrest. This is the classic exam point and it is also true. Exceptions exist — obvious lethal injury, a frozen chest wall that cannot be compressed, or a documented avalanche burial with an obstructed airway beyond a defined duration. `UNVERIFIED — the specific exceptions and the potassium threshold used to determine futility require verification against ANZCOR.`

> [!danger] Handle the severely hypothermic patient gently
> The cold myocardium is exquisitely irritable, and **rough handling, sudden movement or careless positioning can precipitate ventricular fibrillation.** Move the patient horizontally and gently. Avoid unnecessary procedures.

> [!warning] Drugs and defibrillation lose effectiveness when cold
> Below a certain core temperature the heart responds poorly to defibrillation and to resuscitation drugs, and drug metabolism is so slowed that repeated dosing causes accumulation and toxicity on rewarming. Guidelines therefore modify both. `UNVERIFIED — the temperature thresholds and the modified drug and shock intervals require verification against ANZCOR Guideline 11.`

> [!tip] The Osborn (J) wave
> A positive deflection at the QRS-ST junction, most prominent in the inferolateral leads, characteristic of hypothermia and becoming more prominent as temperature falls. Other ECG findings: bradycardia, prolongation of all intervals, atrial fibrillation, and ventricular arrhythmia. Shivering artefact frequently obscures the trace.

> [!warning] Afterdrop and rescue collapse
> **Afterdrop** — core temperature continues to fall after rewarming begins, as cold peripheral blood returns centrally. **Rescue collapse** — cardiovascular collapse on or shortly after extrication, related to loss of the hydrostatic support of water in immersion and to the same peripheral-to-core shift. Both argue for gentle handling, horizontal extrication, and rewarming the core preferentially over the periphery.

**S/Smx:** Shivering initially, then its absence as temperature falls further. Confusion, apathy, slurred speech, ataxia, "paradoxical undressing" in some, then coma. Bradycardia, hypotension, bradypnoea, dilated pupils, and apparent rigor mortis — none of which reliably indicate death in a hypothermic patient.

**Ix:** Low-reading core thermometer, rectal or oesophageal (*why:* standard clinical thermometers do not read low enough and will simply report "low", giving no information about grade or trend; *what:* accurate core temperature). ECG with continuous monitoring (*why:* identifies Osborn waves, bradyarrhythmia and the ventricular irritability that determines handling; *what:* J waves, rhythm). Bedside glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia both causes hypothermia and results from exhausted glycogen; *what:* low glucose). UEC with potassium (*why:* potassium is used in assessing futility in hypothermic arrest and rises with cell death; *what:* potassium). VBG (*why:* acidosis and lactate, noting that gas machines correct to 37°C and the interpretation of uncorrected values is a recognised subtlety; *what:* pH, lactate). Coagulation profile (*why:* cold-induced coagulopathy is not reflected in laboratory clotting times, which are run at 37°C — so normal results in a bleeding hypothermic patient are misleading; *what:* results interpreted with that caveat). TFTs and cortisol (*why:* myxoedema coma and adrenal crisis present as hypothermia and both are treatable; *what:* raised TSH, low cortisol). CK (*why:* rhabdomyolysis from prolonged immobility, particularly the elderly long lie; *what:* elevation). Septic screen (*why:* sepsis causes hypothermia in the elderly and is a marker of severity; *what:* source). Trauma survey (*why:* the fall that caused the long lie may have caused a fracture, and the hypothermia distracts from it; *what:* injuries).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Remove from the cold, remove wet clothing, insulate, handle gently, and monitor continuously. **Passive external rewarming** for mild cases — blankets, warm environment. **Active external rewarming** — forced warm air. **Active internal rewarming** — warmed humidified oxygen, warmed IV fluids, and in severe cases lavage or extracorporeal techniques. Treat hypoglycaemia. Empirical thyroxine and hydrocortisone where myxoedema or adrenal crisis is plausible.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
**Extracorporeal rewarming (ECMO or cardiopulmonary bypass) is the treatment of choice in hypothermic cardiac arrest** where available, and warrants early discussion and transfer. ICU management.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Address why it happened.** For the elderly patient found on the floor, this means a falls assessment, home safety and heating review, social isolation assessment and consideration of a personal alarm — the hypothermia is a symptom of a wider problem, and treating only the temperature guarantees a repeat. Cross-refer [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]].

---

## 0.6 Frostbite and Non-Freezing Cold Injury

**D:** Tissue injury from cold — **frostbite** involves actual tissue freezing, while **non-freezing cold injury** (trench foot, immersion foot) results from prolonged exposure to cold wet conditions above freezing.

**A/P:** *Frostbite:* Extracellular ice crystal formation → osmotic cellular dehydration → direct membrane injury; simultaneous vasoconstriction and microvascular thrombosis → ischaemia. **A second wave of injury occurs on reperfusion**, mediated by inflammatory mediators, and this reperfusion component is a substantial part of the final tissue loss.
*Non-freezing cold injury:* Prolonged vasoconstriction and endothelial injury without freezing → neurovascular damage, characteristically with prolonged pain and sensory disturbance long after the exposure.

> [!warning] The rewarming rules
> **Rewarm in warm water** — not hot water, not dry heat, not a fire, which cause burns in anaesthetic tissue. **Do not rub or massage** — ice crystals in the tissue cause mechanical damage. **Do not rewarm if there is any risk of refreezing** — a freeze-thaw-refreeze cycle causes far worse injury than remaining frozen until definitive care. `UNVERIFIED — water temperature range and duration for rewarming require verification.`

> [!danger] Rewarming is severely painful
> Anticipate and provide substantial analgesia, often opioid. Patients are frequently under-analgesed during this because the extent of pain is not expected.

> [!tip] "Frostbite in January, amputate in July"
> The final demarcation between viable and non-viable tissue takes weeks to months to declare itself, and tissue that initially looks non-viable often recovers. **Early amputation is avoided** except for infection or compartment syndrome. This is a genuinely counterintuitive point and a reliable exam item.

**S/Smx:** Initially numb, white, waxy, hard tissue in frostbite. On rewarming: pain, erythema, oedema, and blistering — **clear blisters indicate more superficial injury and a better prognosis than haemorrhagic blisters.** Non-freezing cold injury shows a pale, cold, numb limb that becomes hyperaemic, painful and swollen on rewarming, with prolonged neuropathic pain.

**Ix:** Clinical assessment of depth and extent after rewarming (*why:* pre-rewarming appearance consistently underestimates and overestimates in unpredictable directions, so grading is deferred; *what:* blister character, tissue colour and capillary return). Core temperature (*why:* systemic hypothermia frequently coexists and takes priority over the local injury; *what:* core temperature). Advanced imaging such as bone scan or MR angiography in specialist centres (*why:* assesses tissue perfusion and can guide thrombolysis decisions and predict demarcation earlier; *what:* perfusion defects). CK and UEC (*why:* rhabdomyolysis with extensive injury; *what:* elevation). Tetanus status and wound assessment (*why:* infection is the main early complication; *what:* immunisation history).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Treat systemic hypothermia first. Remove wet and constrictive clothing. Rewarm in warm water once refreezing risk is excluded. Substantial analgesia. Elevate, avoid pressure and friction, and do not walk on a frostbitten foot before rewarming unless required for evacuation.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Specialist involvement. Thrombolysis or iloprost within a defined early window is used in some centres for severe frostbite to salvage tissue. `UNVERIFIED — indications and time windows.` Debridement and amputation deferred until demarcation.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Prolonged cold sensitivity, neuropathic pain and hyperhidrosis are common. Prevention advice and, where relevant, occupational assessment.

---

## 0.7 Drowning and Submersion Injury

**D:** The process of experiencing respiratory impairment from submersion or immersion in liquid, with outcomes classified as no morbidity, morbidity, or death.

> [!info] Terminology
> **"Drowning" is the process, not the outcome** — a person can drown and survive. The terms **"near-drowning", "dry drowning", "wet drowning", "secondary drowning" and "delayed drowning" are deprecated** and should not be used; they caused confusion in both research and clinical communication. Use drowning with the outcome stated.

**A/P:** Submersion → breath-holding → involuntary laryngospasm → hypoxia → laryngospasm relaxes and fluid is aspirated → surfactant washout and dysfunction, alveolar collapse, and V/Q mismatch → **profound hypoxia is the central and primary problem, and every consequence follows from it** — cerebral hypoxic injury, myocardial dysfunction, arrhythmia and arrest. The distinction between salt and fresh water has essentially **no clinical relevance** in human drowning, despite the electrolyte theory taught historically — the volumes aspirated are too small.

> [!danger] Drowning resuscitation differs from standard adult BLS
> Because the arrest is **asphyxial**, ventilation takes priority. **Rescue breaths are given first, before compressions**, and in-water rescue breathing by a trained rescuer is recommended where feasible. This is a genuine departure from the compression-first adult algorithm and is a common exam discriminator. `UNVERIFIED — the exact number of initial rescue breaths and the modified sequence require verification against ANZCOR Guideline 9.3.2.`

> [!warning] Do not routinely immobilise the cervical spine
> Cervical spine injury is uncommon in drowning **unless the mechanism suggests it** — diving into shallow water, a fall from height, surfing, a watercraft collision, or signs of trauma. Routine immobilisation impedes airway management and ventilation, which are the priorities. Apply it where the mechanism warrants it, not reflexively.

> [!tip] Always ask why they went under
> Drowning is frequently secondary to another event. **Seizure · arrhythmia — long QT syndrome type 1 is characteristically triggered by swimming, and a family history of sudden death matters · hypoglycaemia · alcohol or drug intoxication, which is involved in a large proportion of adult drownings · myocardial infarction · syncope · hyperventilation before breath-hold diving · non-accidental injury in a child.** Investigating the precipitant is part of the assessment.

> [!warning] Observation and delayed deterioration
> Patients who are asymptomatic with normal observations and a normal examination after a defined observation period have a very low risk of subsequent deterioration and can generally be discharged. Those with cough, abnormal auscultation, hypoxia or any symptom require admission, since pulmonary injury can evolve over hours. `UNVERIFIED — the recommended observation period and discharge criteria require verification.`

**S/Smx:** Cough, dyspnoea, wheeze, crackles, hypoxia, vomiting (very common and an aspiration risk), altered conscious state, and hypothermia — which frequently coexists and is neuroprotective.

**Ix:** Oxygen saturation and ABG (*why:* quantifies the hypoxaemia that is the central problem and identifies developing respiratory failure; *what:* hypoxia, acidosis). CXR (*why:* aspiration and pulmonary oedema, though early films may be normal and it is the trajectory that matters; *what:* infiltrates, oedema). ECG and continuous monitoring (*why:* identifies both arrhythmia as a cause and hypothermic or hypoxic arrhythmia as a consequence, and **a long QT may reveal the precipitant**; *what:* arrhythmia, prolonged QT, Osborn waves). Core temperature (*why:* coexisting hypothermia alters the resuscitation approach and prognosis entirely; *what:* core temperature). Bedside glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia as precipitant; *what:* low glucose). UEC and VBG with lactate (*why:* severity of hypoxic insult and electrolyte state; *what:* acidosis, lactate). Alcohol and toxicology where relevant (*why:* identifies the contributing factor and informs prevention advice; *what:* level). CT brain and cervical spine where indicated by mechanism or persisting altered conscious state (*why:* trauma and hypoxic injury; *what:* injury, oedema).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
Rescue with attention to rescuer safety. **Ventilation first** — rescue breaths, then compressions if pulseless. High-flow oxygen. Anticipate vomiting and be ready to suction. Manage hypothermia concurrently, remembering it is protective and that resuscitation should be prolonged. Early consideration of intubation and PEEP for significant pulmonary injury.

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Ventilatory support with PEEP for surfactant dysfunction and ARDS. ICU. Extracorporeal support in refractory cases, particularly with severe hypothermia. **Antibiotics are not routine** — reserve them for aspiration of grossly contaminated water or established infection.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Investigate the precipitant, including cardiac assessment where arrhythmia is suspected and family screening for long QT. **Prevention advice: pool fencing, which is legislated in South Australia, supervision of young children, swimming ability, life jackets, and alcohol avoidance around water.** Neurological rehabilitation for hypoxic injury. **Consider safeguarding assessment in any paediatric drowning** — cross-refer [[P3]].

---

## 0.8 Electrical Injury

**D:** Tissue injury from the passage of electrical current through the body, including low-voltage, high-voltage and lightning injury.

**A/P:** Current passing through tissue causes injury by three mechanisms: **direct electrical effect** on excitable membranes (arrhythmia, tetany, seizure), **thermal injury** from resistance to current flow (which is greatest in tissues of high resistance — bone, and therefore adjacent muscle), and **mechanical injury** from tetanic muscle contraction or being thrown. Because deep tissues heat most, **the visible skin injury dramatically underestimates the deep tissue damage** — electrical injury behaves like a crush injury with small entry and exit wounds.

> [!tip] Alternating versus direct current
> **AC (domestic and industrial mains):** causes **tetanic muscle contraction, so the victim cannot let go**, prolonging contact and increasing the dose delivered. It is more arrhythmogenic at lower currents and characteristically causes ventricular fibrillation.
> **DC (lightning, batteries, defibrillators):** a single large throw, typically hurling the victim clear — shorter contact, but often with associated blunt trauma. Characteristically causes asystole.

> [!danger] Deep injury vastly exceeds surface appearance
> **Rhabdomyolysis, compartment syndrome and acute kidney injury are the major early threats** in significant electrical injury, and the limb can be devastated beneath essentially normal-looking skin. Monitor compartments actively, check CK and renal function serially, and maintain urine output. Cross-refer [[L6]] and [[A7]] Crush Injury.

> [!warning] Who needs cardiac monitoring
> Any patient with loss of consciousness, an abnormal ECG, arrhythmia at any point, high-voltage exposure, a transthoracic current path (hand to hand, or hand to opposite foot), or cardiac symptoms. A well patient after a brief low-voltage domestic shock with a normal ECG and no loss of consciousness generally does not require prolonged monitoring. `UNVERIFIED — the voltage threshold separating low from high voltage, and the monitoring duration criteria, require verification.`

> [!info] Lightning injury is a distinct entity
> Massive current over an extremely short duration, with most passing over the body surface — **flashover** — which is why survival is more common than expected. Features: **keraunoparalysis** (transient limb paralysis with pallor and pulselessness, usually resolving), **Lichtenberg figures** (transient fern-like skin markings, pathognomonic and not true burns), tympanic membrane rupture, cataracts developing later, and **asystole that frequently resolves spontaneously while respiratory arrest persists.**
> This produces the one situation where triage is **reversed**: in a multiple-casualty lightning strike, **attend to the apparently dead first**, because ventilation alone may restore them, whereas in most mass casualty situations they would be triaged last.

> [!warning] Pregnancy
> The fetus is at risk even from apparently minor maternal electrical injury, because amniotic fluid conducts well. Any pregnant woman with an electrical injury needs obstetric assessment and fetal monitoring.

**S/Smx:** Entry and exit wounds, burns, arrhythmia, loss of consciousness, seizure, limb pain and swelling, weakness or paralysis, tympanic membrane rupture, and associated blunt trauma from being thrown or falling.

**Ix:** ECG and cardiac monitoring per the criteria above (*why:* arrhythmia is the immediate life threat and may be delayed; *what:* arrhythmia, ischaemic change, prolonged QT). **CK and serial UEC** (*why:* rhabdomyolysis from deep muscle injury is the principal cause of the renal failure that follows, and the surface wounds give no indication of its extent; *what:* markedly raised CK, rising creatinine, hyperkalaemia). Urinalysis (*why:* myoglobinuria appears as dipstick-positive blood without red cells; *what:* positive dipstick, no RBCs). Troponin (*why:* direct myocardial injury occurs, though interpretation is complicated by skeletal muscle injury; *what:* elevation). VBG with potassium (*why:* hyperkalaemia from muscle breakdown is immediately dangerous; *what:* potassium, acidosis). Serial compartment assessment, with pressure measurement where suspected (*why:* compartment syndrome develops beneath intact skin and is limb-threatening; *what:* pain on passive stretch, tense compartments, raised pressure). Trauma imaging where thrown or fallen (*why:* associated fractures including vertebral fractures from tetanic contraction, and posterior shoulder dislocation is classically associated; *what:* fractures, dislocations). Tympanic membrane examination and later audiology in lightning injury (*why:* rupture is common and hearing loss may persist; *what:* perforation). Ophthalmology follow-up in lightning injury (*why:* cataracts develop months later; *what:* lens opacity). Pregnancy test and fetal monitoring (*why:* fetal risk exceeds what maternal injury suggests; *what:* fetal heart rate, CTG).

### 0.8.1 Mx – Immediate
**Ensure the scene is safe and the current is isolated before touching the patient.** A–E, resuscitation per ANZCOR with the reverse-triage principle in lightning. Cardiac monitoring. Aggressive fluid resuscitation with a urine output target where rhabdomyolysis is present — **fluid requirements exceed those predicted by surface burn area, because the burn formulae do not account for deep injury.** `UNVERIFIED — urine output targets and fluid regimens for electrical injury require verification against burns unit protocol.` Analgesia, tetanus status, wound care.

### 0.8.2 Mx – Definitive
Burns unit referral for significant injury. Fasciotomy for compartment syndrome. Debridement of necrotic muscle, often requiring repeated exploration. Renal replacement therapy where required.

### 0.8.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Neurological and psychological sequelae are common and frequently under-recognised — chronic pain, neuropathy, cognitive complaints, anxiety and PTSD. Cataract screening after lightning injury. **Occupational injury requires SafeWork SA notification and workplace investigation.** Cross-refer [[P1]] Occupational Exposure.

---

> Serotonin toxicity and sympathomimetic toxidrome in full → [[F0.1]] 0.4 · Anticholinergic toxidrome → [[F0.1]] 0.2 · Shock physiology → [[F0.3]] · Cardiac arrest algorithms → [[A1]] 0.4 and [[F0.4]] · Burns and chemical injury → [[A7]] · Rhabdomyolysis and compartment syndrome → [[L6]] · Thyroid storm → [[I1]] · Falls and the elderly long lie → [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]]

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Assessment method, mechanism and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every percentage threshold, fluid formula, pH target, time window and dose carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** The Parkland formula and all burns referral thresholds are **omitted, not flagged** — obtain them from ANZBA (Australian and New Zealand Burn Association) and your state burns service. Tetanus prophylaxis comes from the Australian Immunisation Handbook.

---

## 0.1 Burns — Assessment

**D:** Tissue injury from thermal, chemical, electrical or radiation energy, classified by depth and by percentage of total body surface area involved.

**A/P:** Energy transfer denatures protein and causes coagulative necrosis at the point of contact, with concentric **zones of injury**: a central **zone of coagulation** (irreversibly dead), surrounded by a **zone of stasis** (compromised perfusion, potentially salvageable, and the tissue that early cooling and adequate resuscitation are trying to save), surrounded by a **zone of hyperaemia** (inflammation, will recover). **The zone of stasis can convert to necrosis with hypoperfusion, hypothermia, oedema or infection**, which is why the burn seen at 48 hours may be deeper than the burn seen at presentation — and why depth assessment is deferred and repeated.

> [!tip] Depth assessment
> | Depth | Appearance | Blisters | Capillary refill | Sensation | Healing |
> |---|---|---|---|---|---|
> | **Superficial (epidermal)** | Red, dry | **No** | Brisk | **Painful** | Days, no scar |
> | **Superficial partial (superficial dermal)** | Pink, moist | Yes | Brisk | **Very painful** | ~2 weeks, minimal scar |
> | **Deep partial (deep dermal)** | Blotchy red/white | Yes | **Sluggish or absent** | **Reduced** | Slow, scarring, often grafted |
> | **Full thickness** | White, waxy, leathery, charred | No | **Absent** | **Painless** | Will not heal, requires grafting |
> **Reduced sensation and absent capillary refill indicate a deeper burn — the painless burn is the worrying one.**

> [!danger] Superficial burns are NOT counted in the total body surface area
> Simple erythema — sunburn, a scald that is red but unblistered — is **excluded from the TBSA calculation.** Including it inflates the estimate, which then inflates the calculated fluid resuscitation and causes harm through over-resuscitation. This is one of the commonest errors in burns assessment.

> [!tip] Estimating TBSA
> **Rule of nines** in adults — a rapid approximation dividing the body into multiples of nine percent.
> **Lund and Browder chart** in children — required, because a child's head accounts for a proportionally much larger and the legs a smaller share of surface area than an adult's, and the rule of nines therefore systematically misestimates in children.
> **Palmar surface** — the patient's own palm including the fingers approximates a small fixed percentage, useful for scattered or irregular burns.
> `UNVERIFIED — the specific percentages assigned to each body region in the rule of nines, the palmar surface percentage, and the age-adjusted Lund and Browder values are omitted. Use a printed chart, which every emergency department has, rather than recall.`

> [!warning] Burns unit referral — the categories, not the numbers
> Referral is indicated for: burns exceeding a defined TBSA threshold · **any full thickness burn** · burns to **face, hands, feet, perineum, genitalia or across major joints** · **circumferential** burns · electrical and chemical burns · **inhalation injury** · burns at extremes of age · burns in patients with significant comorbidity · burns with associated trauma · and **any burn where non-accidental injury is suspected.**
> `UNVERIFIED — the TBSA percentage thresholds for referral, which differ for adults and children, are omitted. Obtain from ANZBA and your state burns service referral criteria.`

> [!danger] First aid: twenty minutes of cool running water, and it works late
> **Cool running water for 20 minutes is the single most effective first aid measure**, reducing burn depth and improving outcome. Critically, **it remains beneficial for up to three hours after the injury**, so it should be started even in a patient presenting hours later — this is frequently not done because the injury is assumed to be "set". This is standard ANZBA teaching and a reliable exam point.
> **Do not use ice or iced water** — it causes vasoconstriction, deepens the burn and risks hypothermia.
> **Watch for hypothermia** during cooling, particularly in children and large burns. Cool the burn, warm the patient.

**S/Smx:** Pain (or its absence in full thickness), erythema, blistering, eschar, oedema. Assess for inhalation injury: facial burns, singed nasal hair, carbonaceous sputum, hoarseness, stridor — cross-refer [[A2]] 0.2.

**Ix:** Serial depth and TBSA assessment (*why:* depth evolves over the first 48 hours and the initial assessment underestimates in a meaningful proportion, so a single early assessment should not drive definitive decisions; *what:* depth conversion, refined TBSA). Weight (*why:* fluid resuscitation is weight-based and an estimate propagates into every subsequent calculation; *what:* weight in kilograms). Carboxyhaemoglobin by co-oximetry in enclosed-space fires (*why:* standard oximetry reads falsely normal and CO poisoning coexists; *what:* elevated COHb — cross-refer [[A5]] 0.4). VBG with lactate (*why:* a markedly raised lactate out of proportion to the burn suggests concurrent cyanide poisoning from combustion of synthetic materials; *what:* acidosis, high lactate). FBC, UEC, coagulation, group and hold (*why:* baseline before large-volume resuscitation and possible surgery; *what:* baseline values). CK and urinalysis in electrical or deep burns (*why:* rhabdomyolysis; *what:* raised CK, dipstick-positive blood without RBCs). Bronchoscopy where inhalation injury is suspected (*why:* directly assesses airway involvement and grades it; *what:* soot, mucosal oedema, ulceration). Photographic documentation (*why:* enables serial comparison, remote burns unit consultation, and — where non-accidental injury is a concern — a contemporaneous record; *what:* pattern, distribution, demarcation).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
See 0.2.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
See 0.2.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
See 0.2.

---

## 0.2 Burns — Resuscitation and Management

**A/P:** Beyond a threshold TBSA, burn injury produces a **systemic inflammatory response with generalised capillary leak** — not just local oedema. Plasma shifts into the interstitium throughout the body, producing hypovolaemic shock despite total body fluid excess, alongside myocardial depression and increased vascular resistance. This is the "burn shock" that fluid resuscitation formulae address, and it is why a large burn is a systemic illness rather than a skin injury.

> [!danger] The fluid formula is omitted
> `UNVERIFIED — the Parkland (or modified Parkland) formula, the crystalloid volume in mL/kg per %TBSA, the proportion given in the first eight hours, the timing reference point (from time of INJURY, not from time of presentation — this is the part most often got wrong), the urine output targets for adults and children, and the paediatric maintenance additions are ALL omitted from this file. Obtain them from ANZBA and your local burns protocol. This is precisely the category of figure this corpus has previously carried incorrectly.`
> Two principles that are safe to state: the formula gives a **starting point, not a prescription** — fluid is titrated to urine output and clinical response, and both over- and under-resuscitation cause harm. And the clock runs **from the time of the burn**, so a patient presenting four hours late has already lost part of the first period and the calculation must account for it.

> [!danger] Circumferential burns and escharotomy
> A circumferential full-thickness burn forms an inelastic constricting eschar. As oedema develops beneath it: around a **limb** → progressive vascular compromise and compartment syndrome; around the **chest** → restriction of chest wall expansion and ventilatory failure that no amount of oxygen will fix. **Escharotomy is the treatment and it is time-critical.** Monitor distal perfusion and ventilatory pressures actively in any circumferential burn.

> [!warning] Analgesia is systematically inadequate in burns
> Partial thickness burns are extremely painful, dressing changes more so. Use multimodal analgesia with opioid titration, and anticipate procedural analgesia needs. Under-treatment of burn pain is common and avoidable — cross-refer [[F0.4]] 0.9.

> [!danger] Non-accidental injury
> Patterns raising concern in a child: **sharply demarcated "glove and stocking" or "doughnut" scald distributions** suggesting forced immersion · symmetrical burns · burns with no splash marks · **cigarette burns** — discrete, round, uniform · burns to the buttocks or perineum · **delayed presentation** · a history inconsistent with the injury or with the child's developmental stage · a changing history. Any of these mandates escalation and a child protection discussion. Cross-refer [[P3]] Safeguarding.

**Ix:** As per 0.1, with serial UEC, urine output monitoring and weight (*why:* resuscitation is titrated to urine output and daily weight tracks fluid accumulation; *what:* hourly urine output, weight trend). Serial FBC and albumin (*why:* haemoconcentration then dilution, and protein loss from the burn surface; *what:* haematocrit, falling albumin). Wound swabs where infection is suspected — not routinely (*why:* burns are colonised, so routine swabs generate misleading results and drive unnecessary antibiotics; *what:* organism where clinical infection is present).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Stop the burning process. **Twenty minutes of cool running water.** A–E, with early consideration of intubation for inhalation injury — **intubate early rather than late**, since airway oedema progresses over hours. High-flow oxygen. Large-bore IV access, preferably through unburnt skin. Weight-based fluid resuscitation titrated to urine output. Analgesia. Cover with cling film applied in strips, not circumferentially. **Keep the patient warm.** Tetanus status. Contact the burns service early — most Australian burns units will accept a photograph and a phone call.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Burns unit transfer per referral criteria. Escharotomy for circumferential burns. Early excision and grafting of deep burns. Nutritional support, since the hypermetabolic response to a major burn is profound and prolonged. Infection surveillance.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Scar management with pressure garments and silicone, physiotherapy and occupational therapy to prevent contracture, itch management, psychological support, and long-term follow-up. **Sun protection of healed burns**, which pigment abnormally and burn easily.

---

## 0.3 Chemical Burns

**D:** Tissue injury from contact with a corrosive or reactive substance.

**A/P:** The mechanism differs by class, and the difference is clinically important.
**Acids** cause **coagulative necrosis** → the coagulated protein forms an eschar that limits further penetration → injury tends to be more superficial and self-limiting.
**Alkalis** cause **liquefactive necrosis** → saponification of fat and dissolution of protein → **no barrier forms and penetration continues** → alkali burns are typically deeper, progress for longer, and are more damaging than acid burns of equivalent concentration. This is counterintuitive to most people's expectations and is a reliable exam discriminator.

> [!danger] Hydrofluoric acid is a systemic emergency, not a skin injury
> HF penetrates tissue readily and **the fluoride ion chelates calcium and magnesium**, causing profound systemic hypocalcaemia and hypomagnesaemia with hyperkalaemia and fatal arrhythmia. **Death has followed exposure of a relatively small body surface area.** Pain is characteristically severe and out of proportion to the visible injury, and may be **delayed by hours** with dilute solutions — so an initially unimpressive presentation is not reassuring.
> Treatment is **calcium** — topical calcium gluconate gel, and intra-arterial or intravenous calcium for significant exposures, alongside cardiac monitoring and serial calcium, magnesium and potassium. Found in rust removers, glass etching products, and industrial cleaning agents. `UNVERIFIED — calcium gluconate gel concentration, intra-arterial technique and systemic dosing require verification with the Poisons Information Centre on 13 11 26.`

> [!warning] Irrigation rules
> **Copious and prolonged irrigation with water is the mainstay**, and for alkali burns it needs to continue far longer than instinct suggests.
> **Brush off dry powder before irrigating** — some agents, notably elemental sodium, potassium and dry lime, **react exothermically with water** and will worsen the injury.
> **Do not attempt chemical neutralisation** — the neutralisation reaction is itself exothermic and causes additional thermal injury.
> Remove contaminated clothing and protect yourself with PPE — secondary contamination of staff is a real event.

> [!tip] Cement burns
> Wet cement is strongly alkaline. Injury is typically to the knees and lower legs from kneeling in it, develops slowly over hours while the person continues working, and **presents late with a deep alkali burn.** A classic occupational presentation and easily missed because the patient does not connect the exposure with the injury.

**S/Smx:** Pain, erythema, ulceration, eschar, with the severity often evolving over hours. Systemic features in HF and in absorbed agents.

**Ix:** Identification of the agent, ideally from the **Safety Data Sheet**, which is legally required to be available in Australian workplaces (*why:* management is entirely agent-dependent and the SDS gives composition and first aid guidance; *what:* substance, concentration, recommended decontamination). pH testing of the affected area where feasible (*why:* guides how long to continue irrigation, since the endpoint is normalisation rather than a fixed time; *what:* surface pH). **Serial calcium, magnesium and potassium with continuous ECG monitoring in HF exposure** (*why:* the systemic electrolyte derangement is what kills, and it develops after the skin injury is apparent; *what:* falling calcium and magnesium, rising potassium, QT prolongation and arrhythmia). UEC and VBG (*why:* systemic absorption and metabolic derangement; *what:* acidosis, electrolyte disturbance). Serial wound assessment (*why:* chemical burns progress and the depth at presentation underestimates the final injury; *what:* depth conversion).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
PPE. Remove clothing. Brush off dry agent. **Copious prolonged irrigation.** Identify the agent and call **13 11 26**. Specific antidote where one exists — calcium for HF. Cardiac monitoring for HF and systemically absorbed agents.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Burns unit referral — **all chemical burns meet referral criteria.** Surgical debridement. Correction of electrolyte derangement.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Occupational assessment and **SafeWork SA notification** where work-related. Review of workplace handling procedures and PPE. Cross-refer [[P1]] Occupational Exposure.

---

## 0.4 Chemical Eye Injury

**D:** Chemical contact with the ocular surface — one of the few true ophthalmic emergencies where treatment precedes assessment.

**A/P:** As in 0.3, alkalis penetrate rapidly through the cornea into the anterior chamber via liquefactive necrosis, damaging the corneal endothelium, iris, lens and trabecular meshwork within minutes, and destroying the **limbal stem cells** that regenerate corneal epithelium. Loss of limbal stem cells leads to permanent corneal opacification and vision loss. Acids coagulate corneal protein, which limits penetration and generally produces a less severe injury.

> [!danger] Irrigate first — before visual acuity, before history, before anything
> **This is the only ocular presentation where treatment precedes assessment.** Every minute of continued chemical contact causes further irreversible injury. Start irrigation at triage, in the waiting room, with whatever clean fluid is available if sterile saline is not immediately to hand. Documenting visual acuity first is the wrong instinct here.
> Use topical anaesthetic to permit adequate irrigation — blepharospasm otherwise makes it impossible. A **Morgan lens** or IV giving set directed across the eye, with copious volume.
> **Evert both eyelids and sweep the fornices** for retained particulate matter, particularly with lime, cement or plaster — retained particles continue to release alkali and irrigation alone will not clear them.
> **Irrigate until the pH normalises**, then recheck after a delay, since pH can rebound as chemical leaches from the tissue. `UNVERIFIED — the target pH range and the recheck interval require verification.`

> [!warning] Limbal blanching is the prognostic sign
> **A white, ischaemic limbus indicates destruction of the limbal vessels and stem cells and predicts poor outcome.** Paradoxically, a severely injured eye may look **deceptively white and quiet** because the conjunctival vessels have been destroyed — whereas a mildly injured eye is angrily red. **A white eye after an alkali injury is worse, not better.** This inversion of the usual rule is the single most important observation in the section.

**S/Smx:** Severe pain, blepharospasm, lacrimation, blurred vision, conjunctival injection or blanching, corneal haze or opacity, and epithelial defect.

**Ix:** **pH testing of the conjunctival fornix with litmus paper** (*why:* determines whether irrigation has been adequate and when to stop, and it is the only objective endpoint available; *what:* pH before, during and after irrigation, with a repeat after a delay). Visual acuity — **after irrigation** (*why:* baseline for progression and medicolegal documentation, but it must not delay treatment; *what:* acuity in each eye). Fluorescein staining with slit lamp or blue light (*why:* demonstrates the epithelial defect and its extent, which grades severity and tracks healing; *what:* area of uptake). Assessment of limbal perfusion (*why:* limbal ischaemia is the principal prognostic determinant; *what:* extent of limbal blanching in clock hours). Intraocular pressure (*why:* alkali injury damages the trabecular meshwork and causes acute and chronic glaucoma; *what:* raised pressure). Lid eversion and fornix inspection (*why:* retained particulate perpetuates the injury and is missed unless actively looked for; *what:* retained solid material). Agent identification via Safety Data Sheet (*why:* alkali versus acid changes prognosis and follow-up intensity; *what:* substance and concentration).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
**Irrigate immediately and copiously**, with topical anaesthetic, lid eversion and fornix sweeping. Continue until pH normalises and recheck. **Urgent ophthalmology referral for all but the most trivial exposures.**

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Ophthalmology management — topical steroids, cycloplegics, ascorbate and citrate in some protocols, pressure control, and surgical intervention including limbal stem cell transplantation for severe injury.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Long-term follow-up for glaucoma, corneal scarring, symblepharon and dry eye. Eye protection education and occupational review. Cross-refer [[E1]] Red & Painful Eye.

---

## 0.5 Minor Traumatic Wound

**D:** A wound not requiring operative management, where the clinical tasks are excluding occult injury, deciding on closure, and preventing infection.

> [!danger] Examine the wound through the full range of motion the limb was in when injured
> A tendon injured with the finger flexed retracts, and the divided end **will not be visible when you examine the hand extended.** A partially divided tendon may look intact at rest and rupture later. **Explore in the position of injury**, and test each tendon individually against resistance. Missed tendon and nerve injuries in hand wounds are among the commonest and most consequential emergency department errors.

> [!warning] Wounds that should not be closed primarily
> **Bites** — particularly cat bites and any bite to the hand · **puncture wounds**, especially through footwear · heavily contaminated or devitalised wounds · wounds presenting beyond a time threshold · wounds with retained foreign material.
> These are irrigated, debrided and left for **delayed primary closure or healing by secondary intention.** Closing an infected or contaminated wound converts a manageable problem into an abscess.

> [!danger] The "fight bite"
> A small wound over the **metacarpophalangeal joint** sustained by punching someone in the mouth. The patient rarely volunteers the mechanism. **The tooth penetrates the extensor tendon and joint capsule with the fist clenched, and the injury tract seals as the hand opens** — so a trivial-looking 5 mm laceration overlies a contaminated joint. These require exploration, washout, antibiotics and hand surgical involvement. Assume any wound over the MCP joints is a fight bite until proven otherwise.

> [!tip] Foreign bodies
> **Glass is radio-opaque** — plain radiography detects most glass fragments, and any wound caused by glass warrants an X-ray. **Wood, plastic and thorns are radiolucent** — ultrasound is the better modality. A retained foreign body is a common cause of a wound that fails to heal or repeatedly becomes infected, and a common source of medicolegal complaint.

> [!warning] Tetanus
> Assess immunisation status and wound type — tetanus-prone wounds include those contaminated with soil, saliva or faeces, puncture wounds, and wounds with devitalised tissue. Both vaccine and immunoglobulin may be indicated depending on history and wound type. `UNVERIFIED — the vaccination schedule, booster intervals and immunoglobulin indications require verification against the Australian Immunisation Handbook.`

**A/P:** Wound healing proceeds through haemostasis, inflammation, proliferation and remodelling. Contamination, devitalised tissue, foreign material and impaired perfusion all prolong the inflammatory phase and predispose to infection — which is the logic behind irrigation, debridement and selective delayed closure.

**S/Smx:** Assess: mechanism, time since injury, contamination, and the patient's tetanus status, diabetes, immunosuppression, anticoagulation and vascular disease. Examine: neurovascular status distally, individual tendon function, joint involvement, and depth.

**Ix:** Structured neurovascular and tendon examination distal to the wound, documented (*why:* deficits found later are attributed to the treating clinician if not documented at presentation, and more importantly a missed tendon or nerve injury has a far worse outcome when repaired late; *what:* sensation in each nerve distribution, individual tendon function against resistance, capillary refill, pulses). Plain radiography where glass, metal or a fracture is possible (*why:* glass is radio-opaque and retained fragments are a common and preventable cause of poor healing; *what:* radio-opaque foreign body, fracture, air in a joint). Ultrasound for suspected radiolucent foreign body (*why:* wood, thorns and plastic are invisible on plain films; *what:* echogenic foreign body with shadowing). Wound exploration in a bloodless field with adequate anaesthesia and lighting (*why:* the definitive assessment — inadequate exploration is the mechanism by which injuries are missed, and "the wound was too painful to explore" is not an acceptable reason; *what:* depth, tendon, joint capsule, foreign material). Wound swab only where infection is established (*why:* swabbing a fresh wound cultures colonisers and misleads; *what:* organism in established infection).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Haemostasis, adequate analgesia and anaesthesia, **copious irrigation**, debridement of devitalised tissue, exploration, and closure or planned delayed closure. Tetanus prophylaxis. Antibiotic prophylaxis for bites, contaminated wounds, and wounds involving bone, joint or tendon. `UNVERIFIED — antibiotic indications and regimens from eTG Antibiotic.`

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Hand surgery or plastics referral for tendon, nerve, joint or complex wounds. Delayed primary closure where indicated.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Wound review at a defined interval, suture or staple removal at a site-appropriate time, scar advice, and **explicit written return advice for infection.** Cross-refer [[Examination]] Wound Management — Basic Principles.

---

## 0.6 Crush Injury and Rhabdomyolysis

**D:** Injury from prolonged compression of muscle, and the systemic syndrome that follows its release.

**A/P:** Sustained compression → direct muscle injury and ischaemia → myocyte membrane failure with influx of sodium, water and calcium and efflux of potassium, phosphate, myoglobin and creatine kinase. **While the limb remains compressed, these products are sequestered.** On release, reperfusion flushes them into the circulation → **crush syndrome**: sudden hyperkalaemia with arrhythmia, metabolic acidosis, hypocalcaemia, hypovolaemia as fluid sequesters into the reperfused limb, and **myoglobin-induced acute kidney injury** through tubular obstruction, direct toxicity and renal vasoconstriction.

> [!danger] The deterioration happens on release, not during compression
> A patient can be alert and talking while trapped, and arrest within minutes of extrication. **Where possible, establish IV access and begin aggressive fluid resuscitation BEFORE the crushing force is released** — this is the central principle of crush injury management and it inverts the usual instinct to extricate first. `UNVERIFIED — pre-release fluid regimens and the management of anticipated hyperkalaemia at extrication require verification against a current disaster medicine or retrieval source.`

> [!danger] Compartment syndrome — the classic "5 Ps" are a trap
> Pallor, pulselessness, paralysis and paraesthesia are **late findings**, and by the time they appear the limb is often already lost. **Pulses are typically present in compartment syndrome**, because compartment pressure rises above capillary and venous pressure long before it reaches arterial pressure — waiting for a pulseless limb is waiting too long.
> The **earliest and most reliable signs are pain out of proportion to the injury, and pain on passive stretch of the muscles within the compartment.** A patient requiring escalating opioid doses after a fracture or crush is describing compartment syndrome until proven otherwise.
> **Fasciotomy is time-critical.** Where clinical suspicion is high, it should not wait for pressure measurement, and it certainly should not wait for pulses to disappear. Compartment pressures are measured when the patient cannot report symptoms — obtunded, intubated, or under regional block. `UNVERIFIED — compartment pressure and delta pressure thresholds require verification.`

> [!warning] Regional anaesthesia and compartment syndrome
> A dense nerve block abolishes the pain that is the diagnostic sign. This is a real consideration in limb trauma and is why the decision to block a limb at risk of compartment syndrome should involve a senior clinician — cross-refer [[F0.4]] 0.11.

**S/Smx:** Swollen tense painful limb, pain on passive stretch, sensory disturbance, weakness, dark tea-coloured urine, and systemic features of hyperkalaemia and hypovolaemia.

**Ix:** **CK** (*why:* the diagnostic marker of rhabdomyolysis and its trajectory predicts the risk of renal failure — a single value at presentation may underestimate, so serial measurement is needed; *what:* markedly elevated CK, rising or falling). **UEC with potassium, calcium and phosphate** (*why:* hyperkalaemia is the immediate life threat and hypocalcaemia accompanies it; *what:* rising potassium, rising creatinine, low calcium, high phosphate). **ECG with continuous monitoring** (*why:* hyperkalaemic changes precede arrest and the ECG is faster than the laboratory; *what:* peaked T waves, widened QRS, loss of P waves, sine wave). Urinalysis (*why:* myoglobinuria is dipstick-positive for blood with **no red cells on microscopy** — a cheap and characteristic finding; *what:* positive dipstick, absent RBCs). VBG (*why:* metabolic acidosis and rapid potassium; *what:* acidosis, potassium). Coagulation profile (*why:* DIC complicates severe crush injury; *what:* deranged INR, low fibrinogen). Hourly urine output (*why:* the resuscitation is titrated to urine output to maintain flow through the tubules; *what:* output per hour). Compartment pressure measurement where the patient cannot report symptoms (*why:* the clinical signs are unavailable in an obtunded or blocked patient; *what:* compartment and delta pressure). Radiography of the affected region (*why:* associated fractures; *what:* fracture).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
**Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation, ideally started before release.** Continuous cardiac monitoring. **Treat hyperkalaemia urgently** — calcium for membrane stabilisation, insulin-dextrose and salbutamol for shift, and definitive removal by dialysis where severe. Maintain a high urine output. Analgesia. Serial compartment assessment. `UNVERIFIED — all fluid rates, urine output targets, and hyperkalaemia treatment doses are omitted; obtain from local protocol and eTG.`

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
**Fasciotomy for compartment syndrome.** Renal replacement therapy for refractory hyperkalaemia, severe acidosis or established acute kidney injury. Debridement of necrotic muscle. **Avoid nephrotoxins** — NSAIDs, contrast, aminoglycosides — in a patient with established or threatened myoglobinuric renal injury. The role of urinary alkalinisation and mannitol is debated and is a specialist decision.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Renal follow-up, since most myoglobinuric acute kidney injury recovers but some does not. Rehabilitation for the injured limb, management of fasciotomy wounds and their scarring, and psychological support after entrapment, which is frequently traumatic. **Occupational notification where work-related.**

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Inhalation and airway burns → [[A2]] 0.2 · Carbon monoxide poisoning → [[A5]] 0.4 · Electrical injury → [[A6]] 0.8 · Hypofluoric acid systemic toxicity and the Poisons Information Centre → [[A5]] 0.1 · Chemical eye injury in the ophthalmology context → [[E1]] · Analgesia and regional blocks → [[F0.4]] 0.9–0.11 · Non-accidental injury → [[P3]] · Wound management basics in the existing corpus → [[Examination]] Wound Management
